\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\end{split}

(23)

This equation becomes equivalent to the self-consistent model for the high density regime of the ERM-model derived in Ref.
[
25
]
. A thin/ thick line stands for the bare/ dressed propagator, a circle stands for the vertex
V^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}
; see section
C
in the appendix and Refs.
[
25
,
24
]
for all details on the diagrammatic expansion and on the described steps. This calculation shows that the Leutheusser symmetry, which required to consider both contributions to
\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\Xi}}\hskip 1.0pt}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p},s)
in Eq. (
5
), corresponds to the equivalence of planar and nonplanar diagrams in the diagrammatic expansion in Eq. (
23
). As argued in Sect.
III.2
, both sets are required for recovering Rayleigh dissipation
[
25
]
.
If we neglected the non-planar contributions and performed the self-consistent Born approximation (Eq.
7
), we would end up with the high density theory proposed by Grigera and co-workers
[
22
]
.

IV
The un-jammed state

In the un-jammed state, fluctuating stresses (viz. forces) decorrelate with time and decay for
t\to\infty
, viz.
G_{q}^{\perp}(t\to\infty)=0
. This causes a finite value of

\displaystyle\kappa_{q}^{\perp}=\lim_{s\to 0}(s+\xi)\hat{K}_{q}^{\perp}(s)\;.

(24)

In the Newtonian case
(\xi=0)
, the
\kappa_{q}^{\perp}
can be interpreted as non-ergodicity parameter
[
48
]
. A part of the injected momentum does not decay because there are displacement modes which feel no restoring force. The transverse current response remains finite in the Newtonian case, even at long times,
K_{q}(t\to\infty)=\kappa_{q}^{\perp}
.
In the Langevin case, the friction causes a relaxation of the initial transverse momentum with rate
\xi
.
The consequences of Eq. (
24
) are that the kernel
\hat{\mathcal{W}}_{q}(s)=(s+\xi)\hat{W}_{q}^{\perp}(s)
and the generalized vertex
\underline{\boldsymbol{\hat{\Upsilon}}}\hskip 1.0pt(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},s))=(s+\xi)\underline{\boldsymbol{\hat{\mathcal{V}}}}\hskip 1.0pt(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},s)
also yield finite contributions for
s\to 0
.
The final equation for the amplitude
\kappa_{q}^{\perp}
reads

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa_{q}^{\perp}}{1-\kappa_{q}^{\perp}}=\frac{\hat{\mathcal{W}}_{q}(s=0)}{q^{2}}\;,

(25)

with the equations giving
\hat{\mathcal{W}}_{q}(s=0)
in terms of
\kappa_{q}^{\perp}
specified in
Eq. (
67
).
Contrary to the jammed case, all terms in Eq. (
67
) contribute even for
s=0
. However,
\kappa_{q}^{\perp}
becomes small at the transition, and one can neglect all terms of higher order in
\kappa_{q}^{\perp}
then. One ends up with a linear integral equation for the long-time limit of the fluidity, with the coupling coefficients again given by the stability matrix
C_{q,p}

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{W}}_{q}(s=0)=\sum_{p}p^{d-1}C_{q,p}\kappa_{p}

(26)

The zero-frequency fluidity,

\hat{\mathcal{W}}_{q}(s=0)=\mu_{q}^{\perp}/\xi
, sets the (
q
-dependent generalized) shear viscosity
\eta_{q}^{\perp}=(mn\xi)/\mu_{q}^{\perp}
. Note that a Newtonian (
\xi=0
) un-jammed system has a vanishing viscosity which agrees with Bagnold’s considerations of granular media
[
69
,
2
]
. The stationary fluidity can also be related to a length scale
\lambda_{-}^{\perp}=\sqrt{1/\mu_{0}^{\perp}},
which determines the Lorentzian form of the quasi-static current response,
\kappa_{q}^{\perp}\to 1/[1+(q\lambda_{-}^{\perp})^{2}]
for
q\to 0
. In the Newtonian case, it describes the distance over which momenta remain correlated in a ballistic motion. In the Langevin case, it describes the distance over which the externally injected momentum is dissipated into the damped un-jammed system. Considering the un-jammed system to consist of unconnected clusters of cooperatively moving particles, the length
\lambda_{-}^{\perp}
would be linked to the average cluster size
[
70
,
2
]
.

Figure
3
shows the non-ergodicity parameter
\kappa_{q}^{\perp}
in the scalar ERM-approximation for the spring function of finite range,
f^{\Theta}(r)
, and compares it to numerically exact values. There,
\kappa_{q}^{\perp}
describes the spatial structure of all of the modes with vanishing elastic eigenfrequency; see appendix
G
for details. The curves start at
\kappa_{q=0}^{\perp}=1
and decrease to a
n
-dependent plateau for
q\to\infty
. For small
q
, the curves are well described by a Lorentzian parametrized by the length
\lambda_{-}^{\perp}
. Approaching the transition from the un-jammed side, the length increases strongly. As one can see in Fig.
3
, the
F1
-approximation causes quantitative differences but captures qualitatively the shape of the
\kappa_{q}^{\perp}
from the numeric solutions of the ERM model in the un-jammed state. Because of the finite numeric system,
\lambda_{-}^{\perp}
can only be determined below
n^{*}=0.65
.

FIG. 3:
The non-ergodicty parameter
\kappa_{q}^{\perp}
for the spring function
f^{\Theta}
of finite extent
\sigma
(full lines) in comparison with the results of the numerical diagonalization of the ERM model calculated with Eq. (
102
) (symbols). For the results of the numerical diagonalizazion the length scale
\lambda_{-}^{\perp}
is obtained by Lorentzian fits indicated by the dashed black lines. The linear
\epsilon(n)
-relation determined from the sound velocities in the jammed state gives the values in the legend.

In the jammed state, a qualitative difference appears. The numeric solution picks up the rattlers, viz. small particle clusters that are not elastically coupled to others and thus lead to vanishing eigenfrequencies even in the stable solid. These clusters are small, and thus
\kappa_{q}^{\perp}
is rather
q
-independent in the jammed state. The theory does not pick up these rattlers, but identifies the divergence of the correlation length as signature of jamming from below; see Sect.
V.2
.

V
Critical dynamics

The transition between stable and unstable states was identified in Eq. (
9
).
The elastic constants
(v_{q}^{\perp})^{2}
, determined from Eq. (
11
), and the stationary-current amplitude
\kappa_{q}^{\perp}
, determined from Eqs. (
25
,
26
), both vanish when approaching the un-jamming transition; see the Figs.
1
and
3
. Guided by these observations of small quantities, we follow Götze and perform a nonlinear stability analysis
[
71
,
48
,
72
]
to determine the dynamics close to the transition.
Again, the details of this derivation are moved to the appendix, Section
F
. We only go through the main aspects of the Götze stability analysis here in the main text.

V.1
Derivation of the Götze stability equation

We start by
introducing
an auxiliary quantity

\displaystyle s\varphi_{q}(s)=\frac{s(s+\xi)}{(c_{q}^{\perp})^{2}}+(s+\xi)\;\hat{W}_{q}^{\perp}(s)\;.

(27)

It agrees with the fluidity kernel for low frequency and
\xi=0
.

The conceptual idea is, that this quantity
becomes small for
s\to 0
close to the transition. Note, that the shear modulus can be expressed with this quantity as
\hat{G}_{q}^{\perp}(s)=\frac{s+\xi}{s\varphi_{q}(s)}
. In the un-jammed phase holds
\lim_{s,\boldsymbol{q}\to 0}s\varphi_{q}(s)=\frac{1}{(\lambda_{-}^{\perp})^{2}}
, while
\lim_{s\to 0}s\varphi_{q}(s)=\frac{s(s+\xi)}{(v_{q}^{\perp})^{2}}
is true in the jammed phase. We can trivially express the transverse velocity autocorrelation using
s\varphi_{q}(s)
, see Eq. (
80
).
Expanding the renormalized vertex in Eq. (
66
) for small
s\varphi_{q}(s)
leads to the final nonlinear equation to be solved close to the transition:

\displaystyle\begin{split}s\varphi_{q}(s)&-\frac{s(s+\xi)}{(\tilde{c}_{q}^{\perp}(s))^{2}}-\sum_{p}p^{d-3}C_{q,p}\;s\varphi_{p}(s)\\
&=-\sum_{p}p^{d-3}C_{q,p}\;\frac{(s\varphi_{p}(s))^{2}}{s\varphi_{p}(s)+p^{2}}+\mathcal{O}(s^{2}(s+\xi)\varphi_{q}(s))\;.\end{split}

(28)

The transition or critical point is now given when the linearized expansion, viz. the first line in Eq. (
28
), cannot be solved for the small quantity
s\varphi_{q}(s)
. This happens when the critical eigenvalue of the stability matrix
C_{q,p}
equals unity.
Close to the transition,
the stability matrix takes the form
p^{d-3}C_{q,p}=(1+\epsilon)h_{q}\hat{h}_{p}+C^{\#}_{q,p}
with the left
h_{q}
and the right
\hat{h}_{q}
eigenvector to the maximal eigenvalue
1+\epsilon
, where
\epsilon
is called separation parameter. Because of the orthogonality of the eigenspaces,
\sum_{q}\hat{h}_{q}C^{\#}_{q,p}=0
holds. For
|\epsilon|\to 0
,

s\varphi_{q}(s)
is dominated by the critical eigen-direction, which suggests the ansatz

\displaystyle s\varphi_{q}(s)=\sigma s_{*}g(s_{*})h_{q}+\sigma^{d/2}s_{*}X_{q}^{\#}(s_{*})

(29)

with
s_{*}=st_{*}
. Here
\sigma
and
t_{*}=1/\omega_{*}
are two yet unspecified scales.
t_{*}
sets the timescale and
\sigma
rescales the amplitude. Assuming that the largest eigenvalue is simple, one has
\sum_{q}\hat{h}_{q}X_{q}^{\#}=0
. Inserting this ansatz (
29
) in Eq. (
28
)
and contracting with the left eigenvector together with the adopted normalizations

\sum_{q}\hat{h}_{q}h_{q}=1
and
\sum_{p}\hat{h}_{p}\frac{h_{p}h_{p}}{p^{2}}=1
,
leads to the scaling equation

\displaystyle\frac{t_{0}^{2}s(s+\xi)}{\sigma^{2}}+\frac{\epsilon}{\sigma}s_{*}g_{\pm}(s_{*})=(s_{*}g_{\pm}(s_{*}))^{2}\;.

(30)

This holds for
s\to 0
with
\tilde{c}^{\perp}_{q}(s=0)=\tilde{c}^{\perp}_{q}
from Eq. (
11
).
The timescale parameter results from the integral over the inhomogeneous part

\displaystyle\begin{split}t_{0}^{2}&=\sum_{q}\frac{\hat{h}_{q}}{(\tilde{c}_{q}^{\perp})^{2}}\;.\end{split}

(31)

Additionally, demanding all terms in Eq. (
30
) to be of the same order, one finds
\sigma=|\epsilon|
.
For the largest eigenvalue becoming larger than 1, there is no unique positive solution for Eq. (
11
). Thus,
\epsilon>0
corresponds to the un-jammed phase
; it is negative in the jammed states; different subscripts in
g_{\pm}
denote the scaling function in either state. For
(s+\xi)\approx\xi
, Eq. (
30
) is the scaling equation of Götze’s F1-model
[
71
,
48
]
. The un-jamming transition is thus of continuous type as had been suggested by Voigtmann
[
40
]
.

There is an aspect worth discussing.
Close to the transition,
s\varphi_{q}(s)
vanishes in the small frequency limit. The first term on the right-hand side of Eq. (
28
) then features a potential divergence for
p\to 0

[
72
]
. At least in lower dimensions
d<5
.

We show in the appendix, section
F.2
that this divergence is eliminated by the vanishing of the critical left eigenvector in the long wavelength limit,
\hat{h}_{p}\propto p^{2}
for
p\to 0
.
The reason for this is, that a divergence would ultimately lead to a contradiction to the presence of Rayleigh-damping. Hence, the cancellation of the two contributions of
\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\Xi}}\hskip 1.0pt}
is at the heart of this observation, which requires us to go beyond the self-consistent Born approximation. We numerically confirm this in the ERM-approximation in Fig.
5
.

After having obtained the scaling functions
g_{\pm}(s_{*})
, we can investigate the scaling behaviour of important observables in the jammed- and un-jammed states.
We look at the Newtonian case in the main text and leave the Langevin one for the appendix, Sect.
F.3
.

V.2
Scaling laws in the Newtonian-case
\xi=0

From Eq. (
30
), the characteristic frequency scale follows as
\omega_{*}=|\epsilon|/t_{0}
for
\xi=0
. The general solution for the scaling function is

\displaystyle g_{\pm}(s_{*})=\frac{\text{sign}(\epsilon)\pm\sqrt{1+4s_{*}^{2}}}{2s_{*}}

(32)

Note, that
g_{\pm}(s_{*})
is an uneven function in
s_{*}
. Via Eqs. (
27
,
29
), the susceptibility
\hat{\chi}_{q}(s)
becomes a symmetric function in Laplace space in the range of validity of the expansion,
i.e.
after neglecting
X_{q}^{\#}
. This shows that the Götze stability equation (Eq.
30
) considers the fate of the elasticity of the system. One has to choose the
positive
sign of the square root in Eq. (
32
) in order to get a positive spectrum. Recall, that the solution for the jammed phase is denoted
g_{-}
and the solution in the un-jammed phase as
g_{+}
, depending on the sign of
\epsilon
. In the limits of small and large frequencies compared to
\omega_{*}
, one finds the asymptotic expansions:

\displaystyle\begin{split}g_{\pm}(s\gg\omega_{*})&\to 1\\
g_{-}(s\ll\omega_{*})&\to s_{*}-s_{*}^{3}\\
g_{+}(s_{*}\ll\omega_{*})&\to\frac{1}{s_{*}}+s_{*}\end{split}

(33)

Stationary quantities

Using Eq. (
27
), this gives the zero-frequency fluidity in the un-jammed state as
\mu_{q}=|\epsilon|h_{q}
and the length scale
\lambda_{-}^{\perp}=1/\sqrt{h_{0}|\epsilon|}
. The dispersion relation can also be expressed with the critical eigenvector. We find

\displaystyle(v_{q}^{\perp})^{2}=\frac{|\epsilon|}{t_{0}^{2}h_{q}}\>.

(34)

This result is in accordance with the simulations in Ref.
[
12
]
where the authors found
v_{0}^{\perp}\propto\sqrt{\hat{G}}\propto\sqrt{\omega_{*}}
, with
\hat{G}
being their shear modulus.

FIG. 4:
The length scale
\lambda_{-}^{\perp}
and the speed of sound
v_{0}^{\perp}
computed from Eq. (
26
) and Eq. (
11
) (full lines) compared to the results of the numerical diagonalization of the ERM model (symbols). The circles depict the results of calculations with
N=4\times 10^{4}
and the crosses with
N=10^{4}
. The dashed lines depict a linear fit of the theory results with critical densities coinciding at
n^{*}_{c}=0.830
. The dashed dotted lines show a linear fit of the data from numerical diagonalization leading to
n^{*}_{c}=0.70
based on
v_{0}^{\perp}
and to
n^{*}_{c}=0.82
based on
\lambda_{-}^{\perp}
. The inset shows the transition in
\omega^{*}
(see text for details on the calculation). The dashed lines indicate a linear regression of the jammed and the un-jammed side, where the filled symbols depict the data used for the fits. From these fits we find
n^{*}_{c}=0.78
and
n^{*}_{c}=0.95
.

In the ERM-approximation holds
\epsilon\propto\frac{n_{c}-n}{n_{c}}
. Figure
4
shows the results for the length-scale
\lambda_{-}^{\perp}
and the speed of sound
v_{0}^{\perp}
within the ERM-approximation for the
f^{\Theta}
-spring function.
The result obtained from numerical diagonalization of the Hessian displayed in Fig.
4
is compared to the prediction of the theory. As one can see, the prediction for the speed of sound in the jammed phase lies close to the numerical solution of the ERM-model.
The agreement of the data in the un-jammed phase is also good. This is somewhat surprising, since we solved only the F1-model instead of the full model in this phase.

The numerically observed un-jamming transition of the scalar ERM-model resembles the percolation problem. Percolation was discussed with an exponential spring function
[
73
]
, and we found a similar
n_{c}^{*}
for a linear spring function as for
f^{\Theta}(r)
(data not shown).
As discussed in the appendix, Section
G.1
, the system can only support extended modes in the hydrodynamic limit if a cluster of overlapping particles percolates the system. Thus, instead of asking if a finite speed of sound exists (see equation (
11
)) in this simplified model, we can also ask if overlapping spheres of diameter
\sigma
build a system-spanning cluster. This network of bonds is captured by the
\Theta
-spring function. The value for the critical density predicted by the theory
n_{c}^{*}\approx 0.830
then lies close to the value
{n}^{*}_{p}=0.838
found for the percolation in e.g. the three-dimensional Lorentz-gas
[
74
]
. The mapping onto the ERM model in Sect.
III.4
predicts a dependence of
n_{c}^{*}
on the functional form of the spring function. To reconcile the numerical finding presumably requires a more accurate calculation of the vertex.

Vibrational density of states

To consider the vDOS close to the un-jamming transition, we start from Eqs. (
10
) and (
27
) to obtain the expression

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\chi}_{q}(s)&=\frac{\hat{K}_{q}^{\perp}(s)}{s}=\frac{1}{s^{2}}\frac{s\varphi_{q}(s)}{s\varphi_{q}(s)+q^{2}}\;,\end{split}

(35)

and investigate the two cases
\omega\ll\omega_{*}
and
\omega\gg\omega_{*}
with
s=-i\omega+0^{+}
separately:

1)

\omega\ll\omega_{*}
. In this case,
the system is an elastic medium. Thus, the vDOS in this frequency regime follows Debye’s law (
18
) and is determined by the speed of sound given in Eq. (
34
). The Debye frequency
\omega_{D}=k_{D}v_{0}^{\perp}\propto\sqrt{\epsilon}
vanishes at the critical point. This is again the same scaling which has been found in simulations
[
12
]
.

2)

\omega\gg\omega_{*}
. In this case, the Eq. (
29
) and the first line of Eq. (
33
) lead in the leading order of
\sigma
to

\displaystyle s\varphi_{q}(s)=\sigma s_{*}h_{q}=-i\sigma\frac{\omega}{\omega_{*}}h_{q}

(36)

The general relation for the vDOS, Eq. (
17
), and Eq. (
35
) for the susceptibility give the asymptotic result:

\displaystyle\begin{split}D(\omega\gg\omega_{*})\approx\frac{t_{*}}{n2^{d-2}\pi^{\frac{d}{2}+1}\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)}\int_{0}^{k_{D}}dqq^{d-3}h_{q}\end{split}

(37)

Thus, the vDOS is constant for
\omega_{*}\ll\omega\ll\ \omega_{BP}
, where
\omega_{BP}
denotes an upper frequency limit where the asymptotic analysis based on Eq. (
30
) breaks down. The value of the plateau is included in Fig.
4
and does not depend on the distance to the critical point
\epsilon
.

The inset of Fig.
4
additionally shows
\omega_{*}
as obtained from the vDOS
D(\omega)
calculated by numerical diagonalization. Here we estimated
\omega_{*}
as
D(\omega_{*}/\omega_{0})\omega_{0}>0.17
; see App.
G
for details. As predicted by Eq. (
37
), we can linearly extrapolate
\omega_{*}
approaching the transition from the jammed and the un-jammed state. However, due to the finite size of our system
\omega_{*}
never vanishes completely. We thus exclude the values
\omega_{*}
close to the transition from the linear fits and consider
\omega_{*}
as the most imprecise method to calculate
n^{*}_{c}
. The final estimate for the critical density
n^{*}_{c}=0.80(6)
is the average over the four linear regressions extrapolating
\lambda_{-}^{\perp}
,
v_{0}^{\perp}
and
\omega_{*}
, where the two results of
\omega_{*}
are counted as one weight.

Rayleigh damping

Lastly, we discuss the divergence of the Rayleigh-damping in the jammed phase (we set
\epsilon<0
). Looking at equation (
16
), one expects a divergence of the sound attenuation
\propto 1/\omega_{*}^{d/2}
. The damping results from sound modes losing their momentum to the disorder as captured in the non-analytic self-energy (Eq.
14
). We checked that this singularity is compatible with the un-jamming singularity, and recovered the amplitude.
While this agrees with simulation
[
12
]
, the divergence observed there is stronger,
\propto 1/\omega_{*}^{d}
. It is unclear whether this is connected to the quasi-localized modes present in the particle based simulation.

VI
Conclusion

Relying on the Zwanzig-Mori projection-operator formalism, a microscopic self-consistent model for the emergence of enthalpic rigidity was constructed. We started with the definition of macroscopic rigidity as the non-decaying ability to sustain long-wavelength shear forces
[
11
]
. Hence, we looked at the correlation function of transverse displacements or rather their time derivatives
i.e.
the transverse velocities. We found that the macroscopic stability of the system depends of the maximal eigenvalue of a stability matrix and is hence determined by the energy landscape. This is in accordance with earlier works
[
11
,
6
,
5
]
. We confirmed this idea by testing our equations by solving the scalar ERM-model, which exhibits a percolation-like transition if one chooses a function of finite extent to model the particle interactions. Our theory shows agreement with the numerical solution of the
\Theta
-ERM model and predicts a critical density close to the know percolation value. Furthermore, our theory predicts salient features of the jammed and of the athermal glassy phase. In the un-jammed state cooperative velocity fluctuations are correlated of a diverging length
[
75
,
76
,
77
]
.
Importantly, we constructed our theory based on a Langevin approach. But the emergence of rigidity is independent of the friction parameter
\xi
. To capture transport processes in the un-jammed state requires structural rearrangements and thus to go beyond our approximation of a frozen-in structure.

We argue that one has to go beyond the standard self-consistent Born approximation by including non-planar contributions. Otherwise, the theory would not capture the Rayleigh damping of sound or the correct critical dynamics. This important finding is in accordance with the discussions of the Lorentz-gas
[
58
,
74
]
and of the ERM-model
[
25
,
24
]
. We solved the issue by introducing a retarded renormalized vertex
\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{V}}}\hskip 1.0pt
. We had to perform a total of four Zwanzig-Mori steps to obtain its constituting equation. The end of this sequence of projections was indicated by discovering the bare propagator of the jammed phase and by uncovering, where the self-consistent Born-approximation went wrong.

The theory predicts the same critical dynamics close to the un-jamming transition as found in
[
12
]
except for the divergence of the Rayleigh-Damping. A reason for this could be, that we look at the attenuation of a sound mode, but the stronger damping found in
[
12
]
resulted from other vibrational modes, namely quasi-localized modes
[
78
,
32
]
. But these modes are conceptualized to have a vortex-like spatial structure
[
33
,
79
]
. Such vortex-like structures couple different degrees of freedom and are hence likely to be missed by our purely transverse approach. We will generalize our theory to incorporate longitudinal modes shortly.

Since we included non-planar contributions into the expression for the memory kernel, the positivity of the elastic constants and the fact that the solution for
K_{q}(t)
is a bounded monotonically decaying function are not necessarily conserved by our approximations. Nevertheless, first numerical results look indeed promising. We postpone a analytical investigation to a future work.

Appendix A
Technical aspects in the derivation of the self-consistent model

We consider
N
point-particles with positions
\{\boldsymbol{r}\}_{i}^{N}
and momenta

\{\boldsymbol{p}\}_{i}^{N}
(with identical mass
m
) in a volume
V
in the thermodynamic limit
N,V\to\infty
(so that the density
n=N/V
stays constant). The state of the system is specified by the N-particle phase space distribution

\Psi(\Gamma,t)=\Psi(\{\boldsymbol{r}\}_{i}^{N},\{\boldsymbol{p}\}_{i}^{N},t)
,
whose time evolution is
given by the Klein-Kramers equation,
\partial_{t}\Psi=\Omega\Psi
, with:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Omega(\Gamma)=&\sum_{i}\left(\frac{\partial H}{\partial\boldsymbol{r}_{i}}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{p}_{i}}-\frac{\partial H}{\partial\boldsymbol{p}_{i}}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{r}_{i}}\right)\\
&+\zeta_{0}\sum_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{p}_{i}}\cdot\left(k_{B}T\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{p}_{i}}+\frac{\partial H}{\partial\boldsymbol{p}_{i}}\right)\;.\end{split}

(1)

It is a Fokker-Planck equation generalizing Liouville’s equation by including dissipation and random forces. Thermal energy
k_{B}T
sets the noise scale.
For simplicity, the friction coefficient
\zeta_{0}
is local, independent on particle positions
[
80
]
.
Further,
H(\{\boldsymbol{r}\}_{i}^{N},\{\boldsymbol{p}\}_{i}^{N})
denotes the Hamiltonian energy function.
A radial pair potential is considered so that the force on particle
i
equals:
\boldsymbol{F}_{i}=-\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{r}_{i}}H=-\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{r}_{i}}U(|\boldsymbol{r}_{i}-\boldsymbol{r}_{j}|)
.

An externally imposed inhomogeneous (solvent) velocity field
\boldsymbol{v}^{ex}(t)
(taken to be a plane wave with wavevector
\boldsymbol{q}
) couples to the particle momenta and perturbs the systems. The energy changes by a linear coupling to the momentum field
m\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{q})=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\boldsymbol{p}_{j}e^{-i\boldsymbol{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}_{j}}

\displaystyle\delta H=-\frac{m}{V}\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{q})^{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}^{ex}(t)\;.

(2)

Here, we relied on a spatial Fourier transformation with the wavevector
\boldsymbol{q}
. Later, we will also employ temporal Laplace and Fourier transformation, with the Laplace frequency
s
and the (Fourier) frequency
\omega
. We use on the conventions

\displaystyle\begin{split}\text{FT}[f(\boldsymbol{r})](\boldsymbol{q})&=\int_{V}d\boldsymbol{r}e^{-i\boldsymbol{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}}f(\boldsymbol{r})\;,\\
\text{L}[g(t)](s)&=\int_{0}^{\infty}dte^{-st}g(t)\equiv\hat{g}(s)\;,\hskip 14.22636pt\text{Re}\{s\}>0\;,\end{split}

(3)

Since we exclusively work in the wavevector domain, the spatially transformed function is simply indicated by the argument
\boldsymbol{q}
.
Let
\delta\Omega(t)
denote the change in the time-evolution operator which follows from Eqs. (
1
) and (
2
). An adiabatic perturbation,
\boldsymbol{v}^{ex}(t)=\boldsymbol{v}^{ex}_{0}e^{\epsilon t}
for
t<0
(with

\epsilon>0
an infinitesimal constant) and
v^{ex}(t>0)=0
, shall be applied assuming that the system initially was
in a stationary distribution
\psi_{0}.
It is taken in a way that
\ln\Psi_{0}\to-H/(k_{B}T)
holds
T\to 0
in the spirit of Graham’s discussion of the weak noise limit of Fokker-Planck equations
[
81
]
. Then detailed balance holds
[
82
]
. The shift in the distribution function can be obtained, and from it averages linear in
\boldsymbol{v}^{\rm ex}_{0}
. To simplify the notation already now, only transverse modes will be considered. This requires a transverse external field,
\boldsymbol{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}^{\rm ex}(t)=0
, and introduces the transverse velocity field
\boldsymbol{v}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{q},t)
, with

\boldsymbol{v}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{q})=\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}\times(\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{q})\times\hat{\boldsymbol{q}})
(here
\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}=\boldsymbol{q}/q
). The relaxation of a transverse momentum fluctuation is then given by the normalized momentum correlation tensor

\begin{split}\boldsymbol{K}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{q},t)&=\frac{\int d\Gamma\;\boldsymbol{v}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{q})\;e^{\Omega t}\;\boldsymbol{v}^{\perp}(-\boldsymbol{q})\;\Psi_{0}}{\frac{1}{(d-1)}\langle|\boldsymbol{v}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{q})|^{2}\rangle}\\
&=\frac{\braket{\boldsymbol{v}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{q},t)\boldsymbol{v}^{\perp}(-\boldsymbol{q})}}{\frac{1}{(d-1)}\langle|\boldsymbol{v}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{q})|^{2}\rangle},\end{split}

(4)

where the integral extends over the whole phase space and defines the average
\langle\rangle
. The factor
(d-1)
counts the transverse directions in
d
dimensions. From
\Psi_{0}
, one obtains

\langle{\rm v}_{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{q}){\rm v}_{\beta}(-\boldsymbol{q})\rangle=(Nk_{B}T/m)\;\delta_{\alpha\beta}\;.

(5)

Isotropy simplifies the matrix form to:
\boldsymbol{K}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{q},t)={\rm K}^{\perp}_{q}(t)({\bf 1}-\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}})
, where the scalar transverse momentum response function is given by:
{\rm K}^{\perp}_{q}(t)=\frac{1}{(d-1)}{\rm Tr}\{\boldsymbol{K}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{q},t)\}
; here
\rm Tr
denotes the trace. At solidification, momentum correlations change because of the conversion of dissipative stresses into elastic ones. This can be exposed employing the Zwanzig-Mori (ZM) formalism as it allows to identify slow dynamics. We utilize the operator identity

\displaystyle\Big[s-P\Omega P-P\Omega Q\hat{R}_{Q}(s)Q\Omega P\Big]P\hat{R}(s)P=P\;,

(6)

for the Laplace transformed resolvent
\hat{R}(s)=[s-\Omega]^{-1}
to express the system’s dynamics in terms of the reduced resolvent
\hat{R}_{Q}(s)=Q[s-Q\Omega Q]^{-1}Q
. Here,
P
and
Q=1-P
are two projection operators. We start by projecting onto the transverse velocity field,

P_{v}=\ket{\boldsymbol{v}^{\perp}(-\boldsymbol{q})}\cdot\bra{\boldsymbol{v}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{q})}\frac{m}{Nk_{B}T}\,.

(7)

This introduces the transverse force fluctuations via Newton’s equation. It can be formulated with the adjoint of the operator (
1
),
\dot{\boldsymbol{v}}=\Omega^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{v}

[
82
]
:

m\dot{\boldsymbol{v}}(\boldsymbol{q})=\sum_{j}\Big[\frac{-i\boldsymbol{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{p}_{j}}{m}\boldsymbol{p}_{j}+{\boldsymbol{F}_{j}}-\frac{\zeta_{0}}{m}{\boldsymbol{p}_{j}}\Big]e^{-i\boldsymbol{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}_{j}}\;.

(8)

The first two terms can be identified with the kinetic and potential contribution to the divergence of the stress tensor, respectively. It is a force density
\boldsymbol{F}(\boldsymbol{q})=-i\boldsymbol{q}\cdot\underline{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\hskip 1.0pt(\boldsymbol{q})

[
39
,
83
]
. Here and throughout this work a tensor is represented by an underbar. Generally, spatial indices are displayed by greek letters. The kinetic contribution, formally included, in
\boldsymbol{F}(\boldsymbol{q})
plays no role at
T=0
. The third term
-\zeta_{0}\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{q})
is due to the local frictional forces and breaks momentum conservation.
The transverse velocity
auto-correlation tensor has been computed by Hess and Klein
[
46
]
. The result given as Laplace transform can be found in Eq. (
1
), with the microscopic definition of the shear modulus given by

\begin{split}\boldsymbol{\rm G}^{\perp}_{q}(t)&={\rm G}^{\perp}_{q}(t)\;({\bf 1}-\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}})\\
&=\frac{1}{Nmk_{B}Tq^{2}}\,\langle\boldsymbol{F}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{q})\;e^{Q_{1}\Omega Q_{1}t}\;\boldsymbol{F}^{\perp}(-\boldsymbol{q})\rangle\;.\end{split}

(9)

It is determined by the ZM reduced resolvent,
R_{1}(t)=e^{Q_{1}\Omega Q_{1}t}
, with
Q_{1}=1-P_{1}
. Note that because of time-reversal symmetry
P_{1}\boldsymbol{F}(\boldsymbol{q})=0
holds.
Using the explicit form of
\Psi_{0}
(which gives
\boldsymbol{F}_{j}A\Psi_{0}=-k_{B}T\Psi_{0}\nabla_{j}A
for arbitrary variable
A
), the expression of the instantaneous modulus (viz.
{\rm G}^{\perp}_{q}(t=0)=(c^{\perp}_{q})^{2}
) in terms of the pair correlation function
g(r)
established by Zwanzig and Mountain
[
84
]
is recovered; only the potential part survives at
T=0
and gives the high frequency transverse velocity:

\displaystyle(c^{\perp}_{q})^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{m}\int d\boldsymbol{r}\;\frac{1-e^{-iqy}}{q^{2}}g(r)\bigg[\frac{r^{2}-x^{2}}{r^{3}}U^{\prime}(r)+\frac{x^{2}}{r^{2}}U^{\prime\prime}(r)\bigg]\;,

(10)

Note, that
(c_{q}^{\perp})^{2}
scales with
n
as it arises from pair interactions. The time-derivative of the stress enters in an additional ZM projection step using the projector:

P_{2}=\frac{\ket{\boldsymbol{F}^{\perp}(-\boldsymbol{q})}\cdot\bra{\boldsymbol{F}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{q})}}{\frac{1}{d-1}\braket{|\boldsymbol{F}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{q})|{}^{2}}}\;,

(11)

where the normalisation is given by:

\frac{1}{d-1}\braket{|\boldsymbol{F}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{q})|{}^{2}}=Nmk_{B}T\;(q\,c^{\perp}_{q})^{2}\;.

(12)

The ZM-step introduces an instantaneous damping rate

\gamma^{\perp}_{q}=\frac{\frac{-1}{(d-1)}{\rm Tr}\{\langle\boldsymbol{F}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{q})\Omega\boldsymbol{F}^{\perp}(-\boldsymbol{q})\rangle\}}{\frac{1}{(d-1)}\langle|\boldsymbol{F}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{q})|^{2}\rangle}=\frac{\xi k_{B}T}{m(c^{\perp}_{q})^{2}}\hskip 11.38092pt

(13)

with
\xi=\frac{\zeta_{0}}{m}
.
and leads to a new transverse memory-kernel
\boldsymbol{W}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{q},t)={\rm W}^{\perp}_{q}(t)(\mathbb{1}-\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}})
with reduced dynamics
R_{2}(t)=Q_{2}e^{\Omega_{2}t}Q_{2}
containing
\Omega_{2}=Q_{2}\Omega Q_{2}
and
Q_{2}=1-P_{1}-P_{2}
. Note, that this projector takes out the linear fluctuations of velocity, and stress at wavevector
\boldsymbol{q}
. We could additionally project out states which are linear in the density. But this does not make a difference in the following. One has

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\rm G}^{\perp}_{q}(s)&=\frac{(c^{\perp}_{q})^{2}}{s+\gamma^{\perp}_{q}+(c^{\perp}_{q})^{2}\,\hat{\rm W}^{\perp}_{q}(s)}\\
{\bf W}^{\perp}_{q}(t)&={\rm W}^{\perp}_{q}(t)\;({\bf 1}-\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}})\\
&=\frac{-1}{Nmk_{B}Tq^{2}(c^{\perp}_{q})^{4}}\,\langle\boldsymbol{F}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{q})\Omega\,R_{2}(t)\,\Omega\boldsymbol{F}^{\perp}(-\boldsymbol{q})\rangle\end{split}

(14)

Since the kinetic part of the stress tensor gives only rise to contributions vanishing with the temperature, we only consider the potential stresses
\sigma_{\alpha\beta}^{(p)}
in the present athermal case. The potential stresses and its time derivative can be written with a pair of density fields or a density and a velocity field respectively
[
55
]

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ket{\sigma_{\alpha\beta}^{(p)}(\boldsymbol{q})}&=\frac{1}{V}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}X_{\alpha\beta}(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\boldsymbol{q})\ket{\rho(\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{\kappa})\rho(\boldsymbol{\kappa})}\;,\\
\ket{\dot{\sigma}_{\alpha\beta}^{(p)}(\boldsymbol{q})}&=\frac{-2i}{V}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\text{Re}\{X_{\alpha\beta}(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\boldsymbol{q})\}\ket{\rho(\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{\kappa})(k_{\eta}v_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}))}\end{split}

(15)

with the weights

X_{\alpha\beta}(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\boldsymbol{q})=-\frac{1}{2}\int d\boldsymbol{r}e^{i\boldsymbol{\kappa}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}}\frac{r_{\alpha}r_{\beta}}{r}U^{\prime}(r)\frac{1-e^{-i\boldsymbol{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}}}{i\boldsymbol{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}}

obeying the symmetry
\underline{\boldsymbol{X}}\hskip 1.0pt(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})=\underline{\boldsymbol{X}}\hskip 1.0pt^{*}(\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})
and importantly staying finite at the origin
X_{\alpha\beta}(0,0)=\frac{1}{2}\int d\boldsymbol{r}\frac{r_{\alpha}r_{\beta}}{r}U^{\prime}(r)\propto\delta_{\alpha\beta}
. Generally, we use Einstein’ sum convention for the spatial indices. The super-scripted
∗
denotes the complex conjugate. We abbreviated the spatial derivative in the definition of the weights
U^{\prime}(r)=\partial_{r}U(r)
. It is important to notice, that the weights do not depend on any phase space variable and can hence be pulled out of any average. Due to the coupling to the density,
\ket{\dot{\sigma}_{\alpha\beta}^{(p)}(\boldsymbol{q})}
has a finite overlap with the transverse and longitudinal velocity field or respectively with the product state of velocity and density fluctuation:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\braket{\dot{\sigma}_{\alpha\beta}^{(p)}(\boldsymbol{q}),\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{q})v_{\gamma}(-\boldsymbol{k})}\\
&=-\frac{2i}{V}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\kappa_{\eta}\text{Re}\{X_{\alpha\beta}(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\boldsymbol{q})\}\Big(\braket{\rho(\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{\kappa})v_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\kappa})v_{\gamma}^{\perp}(-\boldsymbol{k})\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{q})}+\braket{\rho(\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{\kappa})v_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\kappa})v^{\parallel}_{\gamma}(-\boldsymbol{k})\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{q})}\Big)\\
&=-2i\frac{NVk_{B}T}{m}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\kappa_{\zeta}\text{Re}\{X_{\alpha\beta}(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\boldsymbol{q})\}\Big[(\delta_{\gamma\zeta}-\hat{k}_{\gamma}\hat{k}_{\zeta})\braket{\rho(\boldsymbol{\kappa}-\boldsymbol{k})\rho(\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{\kappa})\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{q})}+\hat{k}_{\gamma}\hat{k}_{\zeta}\braket{\rho(\boldsymbol{\kappa}-\boldsymbol{k})\rho(\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{\kappa})\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{q})}\Big]\;.\end{split}

(16)

The density fluctuation couples the longitudinal and transverse component of the velocities. In the following, we neglect the second term in the square bracket, which represents the coupling to the longitudinal fields. The reason is, that we aim for a self-consistent theory for the transverse fields. We build an enthalpic theory for the stability at the bottom of the potential energy landscape. Thus, we assume the structure to be time independent and hence do we only take transverse modes into consideration. The generalisation with incorporated longitudinal modes is straight forward and work in progress.

All of this suggests the next projector to be built with density and transverse momentum

P_{3}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\frac{\ket{\boldsymbol{v}^{\perp}(-\boldsymbol{k})\delta\varrho(\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{q})}\cdot\bra{\boldsymbol{v}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{k})\delta\varrho(\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k})}}{NS_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}|}\frac{1}{(d-1)}\braket{|\boldsymbol{v}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{k})|{}^{2}}}\;.

(17)

Here
S_{q}=\langle|\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{q})|^{2}\rangle/N
is the equilibrium structure factor. It is connected to the pair correlation function via:
S_{q}=1+n\int d\boldsymbol{r}e^{-i\boldsymbol{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}}(g(r)-1)

[
39
]
. Note, that the structure factor is calculated with the density fluctuations
\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{q})=\rho(\boldsymbol{q})-\braket{\rho(\boldsymbol{q})}=\rho(\boldsymbol{q})-N\delta_{\boldsymbol{q},0}
. We use the projector
P_{2}
to express the fluctuating
\ket{\dot{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\boldsymbol{q})}
by its overlap with structure and time-dependent displacements. This introduces the vertex

\displaystyle\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}=\frac{-\braket{\boldsymbol{F}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{q})\Omega Q_{2}\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{q})\boldsymbol{v}^{\perp}(-\boldsymbol{k})}}{Nk_{B}Tq(c^{\perp}_{q})^{2}\,S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}|}}\;.

(18)

The adjoint of this vertex is given by
\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}=\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{-\boldsymbol{q},-\boldsymbol{k}}=\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}

The vertex is further analysed and approximated in section
E
of this appendix. It turns out to be convenient to express also the high frequency transverse velocity via the static structure factor. Only considering the potential part of the stresses, one finds

\displaystyle\begin{split}q^{2}(c_{q}^{\perp})^{2}=2\frac{(\delta_{\nu\gamma}-\hat{q}_{\gamma}\hat{q}_{\nu})}{(d-1)Vm}q_{\mu}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\kappa_{\nu}\text{Re}\{X_{\mu\gamma}(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\boldsymbol{q})\}S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{\kappa}|}\end{split}

(19)

We express the fluidity
with a frequency-dependent retarded renormalized vertex

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{W}_{q}^{\perp}(s)&=\frac{1}{(d-1)}\,\text{Tr}\left\{\;\frac{1}{N}\,\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\;\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}\cdot\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{V}}}\hskip 1.0pt}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}(s)\;\right\}\\
&=\frac{1}{N}\,\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\;\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}\,:\,\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{V}}}\hskip 1.0pt}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}(s)\;.\end{split}

(20)

Its constituting equation reads

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{V}}}\hskip 1.0pt}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}&(s)=\sum_{\boldsymbol{p}}\frac{m}{N^{2}k_{B}T}\\
&\times\braket{\boldsymbol{v}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{k})\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k})\hat{R}_{2}(s)\,\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{p}-\boldsymbol{q})\boldsymbol{v}^{\perp}(-\boldsymbol{p})}\cdot\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{p},\boldsymbol{q}}\;.\end{split}

(21)

with the resolvent
\hat{R}_{2}(s)=Q_{2}(s-Q_{2}\Omega Q_{2})^{-1}Q_{2}
obtained by Laplace-transformation. Also note the symbol
:
which connecting two tensors renders a scalar.

The Zwanzig-Mori equation for the occurring 4-point function can be re-written to a constituting equation for the renormalized vertex

\displaystyle\begin{split}(s+\xi)&\;\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{V}}}\hskip 1.0pt}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}(s)+\sum_{\boldsymbol{p}}\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\hskip 1.0pt}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p},s)\cdot\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{V}}}\hskip 1.0pt}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{p}}(s)\\
&=S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}|}\cdot\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}\;.\end{split}

(22)

The new memory function is a
d\times d
matrix

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\hskip 1.0pt}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p},s)\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt=-m\frac{\braket{\boldsymbol{v}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{k})\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k})\Omega_{2}\,\hat{R}_{3}(s)\,\Omega_{2}\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{p}-\boldsymbol{q})\boldsymbol{v}^{\perp}(-\boldsymbol{p})}}{N^{2}k_{B}TS_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{p}|}}\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt\approx\frac{\braket{\boldsymbol{F}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{k})\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k})\,\hat{R}_{3}(s)\,\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{p}-\boldsymbol{q})\boldsymbol{F}^{\perp}(-\boldsymbol{p})}}{N^{2}mk_{B}TS_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{p}|}}\;.\end{split}

(23)

In the last line, we again neglected any contributions arising from the longitudinal current. The reduced dynamics in the memory function is specified by
R_{3}(t)=Q_{3}Q_{2}e^{\Omega_{3}t}Q_{2}Q_{3}
with
Q_{3}=1-P_{3}
and
\Omega_{3}=Q_{3}Q_{2}\Omega Q_{2}Q_{3}
. Note ,that
\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\hskip 1.0pt}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p},s)
is a complicated functional of the six-point density correlation
function, since we continue to consider potential stresses only.

Again, considering
T=0
, we assume the structure factor to be static,

i.e.

S_{q}(t)\approx S_{q}(t=0)
. Consequently, the basic time dependent observable is the
velocity-autocorrelation. Thus, we need to go to the next Zwanzig-Mori level, where the occurring memory kernel again depends on the velocities. Hence, we have to perform one last Zwanzig-Mori step with the projector

\displaystyle P_{4}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\frac{\ket{\boldsymbol{F}^{\perp}(-\boldsymbol{k})\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{q})}\cdot\bra{\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k})\boldsymbol{F}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{k})}}{N^{2}mk_{B}T(kc_{k}^{\perp})^{2}S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}|}}\;.

(24)

where we approximated the overlap by its diagonal contribution

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{1}{d-1}\braket{|\boldsymbol{F}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{k})\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k})|{}^{2}}\approx N^{2}mk_{B}T(kc_{k}^{\perp})^{2}S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}|}\;.\end{split}

(25)

This gives the Zwanzig-Mori equation for
\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\hskip 1.0pt}
in terms of a new memory kernel
\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\Xi}}\hskip 1.0pt}

\displaystyle\begin{split}s&\,\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\hskip 1.0pt}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p},s)+\sum_{\boldsymbol{b}}\;\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\Xi}}\hskip 1.0pt}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{b},s)\cdot\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\hskip 1.0pt}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{p},s)\\
&=(kc_{k}^{\perp})^{2}(\underline{\boldsymbol{1}}\hskip 1.0pt-\hat{\boldsymbol{k}}\hat{\boldsymbol{k}})\;\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p}}\;.\end{split}

(26)

Here, we have neglected a non-zero
frequency matrix, which enters the Zwanzig-Mori equation for the
\underline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\hskip 1.0pt
-memory function and which
is proportional to the Langevin-Damping coefficient
\xi
. This contribution is
also a hydrodynamic kind of damping
\underline{\boldsymbol{\tilde{\gamma}}}\hskip 1.0pt(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p})
, which vanishes with
k_{B}T
and is hence indeed negligible for
T\to 0
. The frequency matrix reads

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{\underline{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}\hskip 1.0pt}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p})&=-\frac{\braket{\boldsymbol{F}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{k})\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k})\Omega_{3}\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{p}-\boldsymbol{q})\boldsymbol{F}^{\perp}(-\boldsymbol{p})}}{N^{2}mk_{B}T(kc_{k}^{\perp})^{2}S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}|}}\\
=&\frac{\xi k_{B}T}{m(c_{k}^{\perp})^{2}}\Big(\underline{\boldsymbol{\delta}}\hskip 1.0pt^{\perp}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p}}S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}|}-\frac{\braket{\underline{\boldsymbol{Y}}\hskip 1.0pt(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})}\braket{\underline{\boldsymbol{Y}}\hskip 1.0pt(-\boldsymbol{p},-\boldsymbol{q})}}{mk^{2}}\Big)\;,\end{split}

with
\underline{\boldsymbol{Y}}\hskip 1.0pt(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})=\frac{(\mathbb{1}-\hat{\boldsymbol{k}}\hat{\boldsymbol{k}})}{Nc_{q}^{\perp}}\cdot\sum_{i}\nabla_{i}e^{-i\boldsymbol{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}_{i}}\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k})\boldsymbol{F}^{\perp}(-\boldsymbol{q})
.

Abbreviating the reduced resolvent by
\hat{R}_{4}(s)=Q_{4}Q_{3}Q_{2}(s-\Omega_{4})^{-1}Q_{2}Q_{3}Q_{4}
with
\Omega_{4}=Q_{4}\Omega_{3}Q_{4}
and
Q_{4}=1-P_{4}
gives

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\Xi}}\hskip 1.0pt}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p},s)\\
&=-\frac{\braket{\boldsymbol{F}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{k})\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k})\Omega_{3}\,\hat{R}_{4}(s)\,\Omega_{3}\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{p}-\boldsymbol{q})\boldsymbol{F}^{\perp}(-\boldsymbol{p})}}{N^{2}mk_{B}T(pc_{p}^{\perp})^{2}S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{p}|}}\end{split}

(27)

This is our last exact result. Now, we aim to get closure and to express
\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\Xi}}\hskip 1.0pt}
in terms of the transverse velocity auto-correlation
K^{\perp}_{q}
, the static structure factor
S_{q}
and the bare vertex
\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}
.

We start again by only considering the potential part of the stress tensor. Additionally, we approximate
Q_{4}\Omega_{3}\ket{F_{\alpha}}\approx 0
. This approximation is justified since the time derivative of the fluctuating forces can be expressed via the current density-fluctuations. After neglecting the longitudinal part of the current, one arrives at

\displaystyle\begin{split}Q_{3}Q_{2}\Omega Q_{2}&\ket{\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{p}-\boldsymbol{q})\boldsymbol{F}^{\perp}(-\boldsymbol{p})}\\
&\approx\ket{Q_{3}\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{p}-\boldsymbol{q})(\Omega\boldsymbol{F}^{\perp}(-\boldsymbol{p}))}\;.\end{split}

(28)

This gives

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\Xi}}\hskip 1.0pt}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p},s)\approx&\frac{-\bra{\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k})[\Omega^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{F}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{k})}Q_{3}R_{4}(s)Q_{3}\ket{\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{p}-\boldsymbol{q})[\Omega\boldsymbol{F}^{\perp}(-\boldsymbol{p})]}}{N^{2}mk_{B}T(pc_{p}^{\perp})^{2}S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{p}|}}\\
\approx&-\frac{\bra{(\Omega^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{F}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{k}))}\otimes\bra{\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k})}Q_{3}R_{4}(s)Q_{3}\ket{\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{p}-\boldsymbol{q})}\otimes\ket{(\Omega\boldsymbol{F}^{\perp}(-\boldsymbol{p}))}}{N^{2}mk_{B}T(pc_{p}^{\perp})^{2}S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{p}|}}\\
=&-\frac{\bra{(\Omega^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{F}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{k}))}(Q_{3}+P_{3})\otimes\bra{\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k})}Q_{3}R_{4}(s)Q_{3}\ket{\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{p}-\boldsymbol{q})}\otimes(Q_{3}+P_{3})\ket{(\Omega\boldsymbol{F}^{\perp}(-\boldsymbol{p}))}}{N^{2}mk_{B}T(pc_{p}^{\perp})^{2}S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{p}|}}\\
\underset{\underline{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\hskip 1.0pt=\underline{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\hskip 1.0pt^{(p)}}{=}&-\frac{mk(c_{k}^{\perp})^{2}}{N^{2}k_{B}TpS_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{p}|}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}}\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\braket{\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k})Q_{3}R_{4}(s)Q_{3}\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{p}-\boldsymbol{q})\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{p})\boldsymbol{v}(-\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime})}\cdot\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{p}}\\
\approx&-\frac{(c_{k}^{\perp})^{2}}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}}\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}}\cdot K_{p^{\prime}}^{\perp}(s)\Big[S_{|\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}|}\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p}}+\frac{k}{p}S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}|}\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{p}}\Big]\cdot\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{p}}\end{split}

(29)

We assume in the second line, that the three point state is a product state. This is indicated by the sign
\otimes
. A pair state
\ket{AB}
is a called a product state
\ket{A}\otimes\ket{B}
if and only if
\braket{AB,(AB)^{*}}=\braket{A,A^{*}}\braket{B,B^{*}}
holds. The six-point correlation function was decomposed into the product of pair-correlators and they were dressed in the last line. In this step, we also relied on translation
invariance to get rid of one integral:
\braket{\boldsymbol{v}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})|R(s)|\boldsymbol{v}^{\perp}(-\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime})}\propto\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}}
. Additionally, translation invariance leads to the occurrence of the Kronecker-deltas in the square bracket.

If we neglect the second term in the square bracket in (
29
), we would perform the diagonalization step in the standard mode-coupling procedure
[
61
,
48
]
and reproduce a qualitatively wrong approximation, since it leads to the incorrect sound attenuation and wrong predictions for the critical dynamics
[
63
,
22
,
23
,
24
,
25
]
. Such an approximation is equivalent to setting
\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\hskip 1.0pt}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p},s)\approx\underline{\boldsymbol{\delta}}\hskip 1.0pt^{\perp}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p}}k^{2}G_{q}^{\perp}(s)
and
\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\Xi}}\hskip 1.0pt}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p},s)\approx W_{q}^{\perp}\underline{\boldsymbol{\delta}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p}}^{\perp}
. We call this a self-consistent Born approximation
[
60
,
59
]
.

For readability, we write

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\Xi}}\hskip 1.0pt}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p},t)\\
&\approx(c_{k}^{\perp})^{2}\left\{\hat{{M}}_{k}(s)\,\underline{\boldsymbol{\delta}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p}}^{\perp}+\frac{1}{N}\tilde{\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p})K^{\perp}_{|\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{p}-\boldsymbol{q}|}(t)\right\}\;,\end{split}

(30)

with

\begin{split}M_{k}(t)&=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{b}}\;\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{b}}:\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{k}}^{\dagger}\;S_{|\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{b}|}\;K^{\perp}_{b}(t)\;.\\
\tilde{\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p})&=\frac{k}{p}\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p}+\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{q}}\cdot\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{p}-\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{p}}^{\dagger}S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}|}\;.\end{split}

(31)

After multiplying the equation (
26
) with the renormalized vertex
\tilde{\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{V}}}\hskip 1.0pt}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{p}}(s)
and integrating over
\boldsymbol{p}
, one finds with Eq. (
26
) (identical to Eq. (
22
))

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{W}_{q}^{\perp}(s)&=\frac{1}{N}\,\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\;\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}\,:\,\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{V}}}\hskip 1.0pt}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}(s)\\
\sum_{\boldsymbol{p}}\left[\Big(s(s+\xi)+p^{2}(c_{p}^{\perp})^{2}\Big)\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p}}+(s+\xi)\;\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\Xi}}\hskip 1.0pt}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p},s)\right]\cdot\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{V}}}\hskip 1.0pt}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{p}}(s)&=\sum_{\boldsymbol{p}}\left[s\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p}}+\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\Xi}}\hskip 1.0pt}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p},s)\right]\cdot\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{p},\boldsymbol{q}}S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{p}|}\;.\end{split}

(32)

The equations (
1
), (
14
), (
3
),
and (
32
) constitute our self-consistent model. Since we did not perform the standard MCT diagonalization step when inserting the two-point projection operator
P_{3}
in the equation for the fluidity (
20
), a few explanatory comments are in order:

a)

The derived model goes beyond standard MCT, by explicitly not diagonalizing the matrix element occurring in the first equation for the fluidity (
20
). Our new approach may be considered a
non-planar self-consistent current response theory
. The notion
non-planar
has its origin in a diagrammatic interpretation of our equations, which becomes applicable in an high density approximation deep in the jammed state. We comment on this is the sections
III.4
and
C
.

b)

The sequence of Zwanzig-mori steps was stopped, when it became clear, where the standard MCT goes wrong.
I.e
, the correct level is indicated by where the standard MCT-approximation scheme becomes equivalent to neglecting a (crucial) term. Also, that we have found the bare propagator
[s(s+\xi)+q^{2}(c_{q}^{\perp})^{2}]^{-1}
of a short time or high density expansion at this level, suggests that we have included a sufficient sequence of consecutive ZM-steps.

d)

The essential inclusion of the essential non-diagonal correction terms, leads to the weights of the memory function
W_{q}
not being necessarily positive anymore
[
48
]
. Hence, the positivity of the spectra of the occurring correlation function is also not guaranteed. Nevertheless, the numerical results suggest that this is indeed the case, but a rigorous proof is still missing.

e)

The symmetry of the bare vertex (see section
E
)
\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}^{\dagger}=\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{-\boldsymbol{q},-\boldsymbol{k}}=\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}
implies
\underset{\boldsymbol{q}\to 0}{\text{lim}}\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\Xi}}\hskip 1.0pt}(\boldsymbol{q},-\boldsymbol{k},-\boldsymbol{p},s)=\underset{\boldsymbol{q}\to 0}{\text{lim}}\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\Xi}}\hskip 1.0pt}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p},s)
. Together with the property
\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{-\boldsymbol{k},0}^{\perp}=-\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{0}}^{\perp}
(again, see section
E
), this gives the symmetry of the renormalized vertex
\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{V}}}\hskip 1.0pt}_{0,-\boldsymbol{k}}(s)=-\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{V}}}\hskip 1.0pt}_{0,\boldsymbol{k}}(s)

[
25
]
. This useful property ensures the correct prediction of Rayleigh-damping.

Appendix B
Jammed phase

Transverse stresses can not decay in jammed phase. This gives a finite long time limit of the shear modulus
G_{q}^{\perp}(t\to\infty)=\lim_{s\to 0}s\hat{G}^{\perp}_{q}(s)
. Hence, a susceptibility
\hat{\chi}_{q}(s)=\frac{1}{s}\hat{K}^{\perp}_{q}(s)
arises along with a self-energy (Eq. (
10a
)).
\hat{\chi}_{q}(s)
and
\hat{\sigma}_{q}(s)
are evaluated with the time-integrated vertex
\underline{\boldsymbol{\hat{\mathcal{U}}}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}(s)=\frac{1}{s}\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{V}}}\hskip 1.0pt}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}(s)
.
Writing
\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{m}}\hskip 1.0pt}_{k}(s)=\frac{\hat{M}_{k}(s)(\mathbb{1}-\hat{\boldsymbol{k}}\hat{\boldsymbol{k}})}{s}
, equation (
32
) becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left[\Big(s(s+\xi)+k^{2}(c_{k}^{\perp})^{2}\Big)+s(s+\xi)\;(c_{k}^{\perp})^{2}\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{m}}\hskip 1.0pt}_{k}(s)\right]\cdot\underline{\boldsymbol{\hat{\mathcal{U}}}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}(s)+\;\frac{s(s+\xi)(c_{k}^{\perp})^{2}}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{p}}\underline{\boldsymbol{\tilde{V}}}\hskip 1.0pt(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p})\cdot\underline{\boldsymbol{\hat{\mathcal{U}}}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{p}}(s)\;\hat{\chi}_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{p}|}(s)=\\
=&\left[1+(c_{k}^{\perp})^{2}\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{m}}\hskip 1.0pt}_{k}(s)\right]\cdot\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}|}+(c_{k}^{\perp})^{2}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{p}}\underline{\boldsymbol{\tilde{V}}}\hskip 1.0pt(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p})\cdot\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{p},\boldsymbol{q}}^{\dagger}\chi_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{p}|}(s)S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{p}|}\;.\end{split}

(33)

B.1
Dispersion-relation

The residual transverse stresses lead to finite restoring forces in the long time limit, whose small wavevector limit defines the speed of sound. We introduce the transverse renormalized dispersion relation as
(v_{q}^{\perp})^{2}=G_{q}^{\perp}(t\to\infty)=\lim_{s\to 0}s\hat{G}_{q}^{\perp}(s)
. The speed of sound is then given by
v_{0}^{\perp}
. We solve our self-consistent model for
s=0
in this paragraph and hence derive an equation for the transverse speed of sound.

Since the susceptibility stays finite for vanishing frequency in the jammed state, the equation for the renormalized vertex can be exactly solved

\displaystyle\begin{split}k^{2}\underline{\boldsymbol{\hat{\mathcal{U}}}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}(s=0)&=\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{p}|}+\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{m}}\hskip 1.0pt}_{k}(s)\cdot\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}^{\dagger}S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}|}\\
+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{p}}&\underline{\boldsymbol{\tilde{V}}}\hskip 1.0pt(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p})\cdot\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{p},\boldsymbol{q}}\chi_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{p}|}(s)S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{p}|}\end{split}

(34)

Thus, the time integrated fluidity at
s=0
can be expressed as a linear integral equation

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{w}_{q}(s=0)&=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}:\underline{\boldsymbol{\hat{\mathcal{U}}}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}(s=0)\\
&=\hat{\Delta}_{q}(s=0)+\sum_{p}p^{d-3}C_{q,p}\frac{1}{(v_{p}^{\perp})^{2}}\;.\end{split}

(35)

with the first order or high frequency contribution
\hat{\Delta}_{q}(s)
and the stability matrix
p^{d-3}C_{q,p}

\displaystyle\hat{\Delta}_{q}(s)=\frac{1}{N}\Bigg\{\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}:\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}^{\dagger}\frac{S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}|}}{s^{2}+k^{2}(c_{k}^{\perp})^{2}}\cdot\Bigg\}

(36a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&C_{q,p}=\frac{1}{N^{2}(d-1)}\int d^{d-1}\hat{p}\text{Tr}\Bigg\{\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\frac{1}{k^{2}}\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}\cdot\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p}}S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}|}\\
&\cdot\Big[\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{p},\boldsymbol{k}}^{\dagger}\cdot S_{|\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{p}|}\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}^{\dagger}+\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{p},\boldsymbol{p}+\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k},}^{\dagger}\cdot\frac{kS_{|\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{p}|}}{|\boldsymbol{p}+\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}|}\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{p}+\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}^{\dagger}\Big]\bigg\}\end{split}

(36b)

We define the coupling coefficients occurring in the equations (
35
),(
36b
) and later in equation (
69
) by integrating over the angular degrees of freedom
\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}
. This leads to

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(v_{q}^{\perp})^{2}=\frac{(c_{q}^{\perp})^{2}}{1+(c_{q}^{\perp})^{2}\hat{w}_{q}(0)}\\
\Longleftrightarrow&\sum_{p}\Big(\delta_{q,p}-p^{d-3}C_{q,p}\Big)\frac{1}{(v_{p}^{\perp})^{2}}=\frac{1}{(\tilde{c}_{q}^{\perp})^{2}}\;.\end{split}

(37)

with the first order renormalised bare sound velocity
\hat{\Delta}_{q}(s)+\frac{1}{(c_{p}^{\perp})^{2}}=\frac{1}{(\tilde{c}_{q}^{\perp}(s))^{2}}
and
\frac{1}{(\tilde{c}_{q}^{\perp}(0))^{2}}\equiv\frac{1}{(\tilde{c}_{q}^{\perp})^{2}}
.
Equation (
37
) is only guaranteed to have a unique, non zero, solution, if the maximal eigenvalue of the stability matrix
p^{d-3}C_{q,p}
is smaller than unity. There are two main reasons why the small eigenvalue scenario corresponds to the jammed case: First of all, the jammed state corresponds to very high densities. The integrated fluidity vanishes with
1/n
in the infinitely densely packed system. Secondly, the speed of sound would be negative if the jammed state corresponded to all eigenvalues being larger than one. The eigenvalues are controlled by the density correlation functions. The solution of our model close to the transition is analysed in chapter
V
. It is important to notice, that the stability of the system is independent of
\xi
.

Rotational invariance implies
C_{0,p\to 0}\propto p^{2}
and
C_{q\to 0,0}\propto q^{2}
, since
C_{0,0}=0
holds, as we will show in the next subsection. It is noteworthy, that the equation for the fluidity in the jammed state basically reduces to the F1-model, which is extensively studied in the literature
[
48
]
.

B.2
Rayleigh-damping

We set
\xi=0
in this section, in order to study the sound attenuation induced by the disorder.
Evaluating the self-energy on the imaginary axis
\sigma_{q}(s=-i\omega+0^{+})
leads to an imaginary part in the inverse susceptibility, which, in the time domain, gives rise to an exponential decay of the correlation function. Hence, this imaginary part is associated with the damping. This non-trivial sound attenuation arises from a non-analyticity of the propagator at the sound pole
\omega=v_{q}^{\perp}q
for
\omega\to 0
. We prove in this section, that the damping is Rayleigh-like,
i.e

\text{Im}\{\sigma_{q}(s=-i\omega+0^{+})\}\propto\omega q^{4}
in
d=3
for
\omega\to 0
, by expanding the time integrated fluidity in powers in
s
for
s\to 0.
The zeroth order gives the dispersion relation. As we will see, the next leading order is in accordance with Rayleigh-damping.

For
s=-i\omega+0^{+}\to 0
, the imaginary part of he self-energy can be written as

\displaystyle\text{Im}\{\hat{\sigma}_{q}(s\to 0)\}\Big|_{s=-i\omega+i0^{+}}=q^{2}(v_{q}^{\perp})^{4}\text{Im}\{\hat{w}_{q}(s=-i\omega+0^{+})\}\;.

(38)

One can identify three different contributions to the residual self-energy
\hat{\sigma}_{q}(s)-\hat{\sigma}_{q}(0)
:

i)

The first contribution arises from the Born-term of the self-energy, which dominates deep in the jammed phase. Writing

\displaystyle C^{(0)}_{q,k}=\frac{1}{N}\int d^{d-1}\hat{k}\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}:\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}^{\dagger}S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}|}\;,

(39)

the residual contribution reads in the thermodynamic limit

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\hat{w}_{q}^{(1)}(s\to 0)-\hat{w}_{q}^{(1)}(0)\\
&=\lim_{s\to 0}\int_{0}^{\infty}dkk^{d-1}C^{(0)}_{q,k}\left[\frac{1}{s^{2}+k^{2}(c_{k}^{\perp})^{2}}-\frac{1}{k^{2}(c_{k}^{\perp})^{2}}\right]\;.\\
&\approx\lim_{s\to 0}\int_{0}^{\infty}dkk^{d-1}C^{(0)}_{q,k}\left[\frac{1}{s^{2}+k^{2}(c_{0}^{\perp})^{2}}-\frac{1}{k^{2}(c_{0}^{\perp})^{2}}\right]\\
&\overset{x=\frac{k{c_{0}}^{\perp}}{s}}{=}\frac{1}{s^{2}}\left(\frac{s}{c_{0}^{\perp}}\right)^{d}\int_{0}^{\infty}dxx^{d-1}C^{(0)}_{q,s\frac{x}{v_{0}^{\perp}}}\left[\frac{1}{1+x^{2}}-\frac{1}{x^{2}}\right]\\
&\propto\frac{\beta^{1,1}}{(k_{D}c_{0}^{\perp})^{d}}s^{d-2}q^{2}+\frac{\beta^{1,2}}{(k_{D}c_{0}^{\perp})^{d}}\frac{s^{d}}{(c_{0}^{\perp})^{2}}\;.\end{split}

(40)

In the second line did we use, that the small
k
regime mainly contributes to the residual part of the self-energy. In the last line did we rely on the behaviour of the vertex
C^{(0)}_{q\to 0,0}\propto q^{2}
and
C^{(0)}_{q=0,k\to 0}\propto k^{2}
. Hence, the Born term indeed gives rise to Rayleigh-damping
\sigma_{q}^{(0)}(s\to 0)\propto q^{4}s^{d-2}
around the sound pole
\omega=qv_{q}^{\perp}
. We added the Debye-wavevector to render the coefficients unitless scalars. One has
[C_{q,p}^{(0)}]=[C_{q,p}]=[q]^{2-d}=q^{2}/k_{D}^{d}]
. This gives
[\hat{w}_{q}(s)]=[\frac{q^{2}}{w^{2}}]
and this is consistent with the requirement
[(c_{q}^{\perp})^{2}\hat{w}_{q}(s)]=1.

ii)

The second contribution arises from the the self-consistent term with the stability matrix determining the weights. For
q,s\to 0
and one finds

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\lim_{q\to 0}\Big[\hat{w}_{q}^{(2)}(s\to 0)-\hat{w}_{q}^{(2)}(0)\Big]\\
&=\lim_{q,s\to 0}\int_{0}^{\infty}dpp^{d-1}C_{q,p}\left[\frac{1}{s^{2}+p^{2}(v_{p}^{\perp})^{2}}-\frac{1}{p^{2}(v_{p}^{\perp})^{2}}\right]\\
&\lim_{q,s\to 0}\int_{0}^{\infty}dpp^{d-1}C_{q,p}\left[\frac{1}{s^{2}+p^{2}(v_{0}^{\perp})^{2}}-\frac{1}{p^{2}(v_{0}^{\perp})^{2}}\right]\\
&\overset{x=p\frac{{v_{0}}^{\perp}}{s}}{=}\frac{1}{s^{2}}\left(\frac{s}{v_{0}^{\perp}}\right)^{d}\int_{0}^{\infty}dxx^{d-1}C_{q,s\frac{x}{v_{0}^{\perp}}}\left[\frac{1}{1+x^{2}}-\frac{1}{x^{2}}\right]\\
&\propto\frac{\beta^{2,1}}{(k_{D}v_{0}^{\perp})^{d}}s^{d-2}q^{2}+\frac{\beta^{2,2}}{k_{D}^{d}(v_{0}^{\perp})^{d+2}}s^{d}\;.\end{split}

(41)

Here, the dimensionless coefficients depend on the number density
n^{*}
as
\frac{1}{n^{*}}
.
The previous equation holds, since the stability matrix vanishes in the hydrodynamic limit due to the cancellation of the planar and non-planar terms:

\displaystyle\begin{split}C_{0,0}&=\frac{1}{N^{2}(d-1)}\int d^{d-1}\hat{p}\text{Tr}\Bigg\{\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\frac{1}{k^{2}}\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{0,\boldsymbol{k}}\cdot\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{k},0}S_{k}\\
&\cdot\Big[\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{0,\boldsymbol{k}}^{\dagger}\cdot S_{k}\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{k},0}^{\dagger}+\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{0,-\boldsymbol{k}}^{\dagger}\cdot S_{k}\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{-\boldsymbol{k},0}^{\dagger}\Big]\bigg\}=0\;.\end{split}

(42)

This proves that the contribution is at least Rayleigh-like. It is important to notice, how crucial the inclusion of the non-planar terms is: A standard MCT-approximation which involves a diagonalization already when determining the fluidity, and which is essentially a self-consistent Born approximation or a planar theory, predicts a hydrodynamic damping
\propto q^{2}s
instead of
q^{4}s
in
d=3.

Note, that we have set
\frac{(c_{k}^{\perp})^{2}}{s^{2}+k^{2}(c_{k}^{\perp})^{2}}\approx\frac{1}{k^{2}}
. This approximation is justified since the contribution of this term to the residual self-energy is Rayleigh-like anyways. This can be proven in similar fashion to point [i)].

iii)

The last contribution arises from

\displaystyle\hat{w}_{q}^{(3)}(s)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}:\underline{\boldsymbol{\hat{\mathcal{U}}}}\hskip 1.0pt^{(3)}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}(s)

(43)

with

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\underline{\boldsymbol{\hat{\mathcal{U}}}}\hskip 1.0pt^{(3)}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}(s)=-\frac{s^{2}}{k^{2}}\underline{\boldsymbol{m}}\hskip 1.0pt_{k}(s)\cdot\underline{\boldsymbol{\hat{\mathcal{U}}}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}(s)\\
&-\frac{s^{2}}{k^{2}N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{p}}\underline{\boldsymbol{\tilde{V}}}\hskip 1.0pt(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p})\cdot\underline{\boldsymbol{\hat{\mathcal{U}}}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{p}}(s)\chi_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{p}|}(s)\;.\end{split}

(44)

Expanding
\underline{\boldsymbol{\hat{\mathcal{U}}}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{p}}(s)
on the right hand side by inserting equation (
34
) and then performing again the steps [i)], [ii)] and [iii)] leads immediately to the conclusion, that the contribution to the self-energy is at least of order
\mathcal{O}(q^{2}s^{d})
and hence in accordance with Rayleigh-damping.

The calculations (i),(ii) and (iii) prove that the sound attenuation is in any dimension in accordance with Rayleigh-damping but the displayed small
s
expansion allows only the calculation of the coefficients for
2<d<4
. One can rely on the Sokhotski–Plemelj theorem in Eq. (
13
) to calculate
the damping in arbitrary dimensions
d>2
for
s=-i\omega+0^{+}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Gamma_{q}^{\perp}(s&=-i\omega+i0^{+})=-\text{Im}\bigg\{\frac{(qc_{q}^{\perp})^{2}}{1+(c_{q}^{\perp})^{2}w_{q}^{\perp}(-i\omega+0^{+})}\bigg\}\\
&\underset{\omega\to 0}{=}q^{2}(v_{q}^{\perp})^{4}\frac{\pi}{2}\Bigg[\frac{C^{(0)}_{q,\omega/c_{0}^{\perp}}}{(c_{0}^{\perp})^{d}}+\frac{C_{q,\omega/v_{0}^{\perp}}}{(v_{0}^{\perp})^{d}}\Bigg]\omega^{d-2}\;.\end{split}

(45)

The coefficients can now be easily obtained by expanding the weights
\underline{\boldsymbol{C}}\hskip 1.0pt^{(0)}
and
\underline{\boldsymbol{C}}\hskip 1.0pt
.

\displaystyle\begin{split}\beta^{(2,1)}&=k_{D}^{d}\lim_{q,p\to 0}\frac{1}{q^{2}}C_{q,p}\;,\\
\hskip 28.45274pt\beta^{(2,2)}&=k_{D}^{d}\lim_{q,p\to 0}\frac{1}{p^{2}}C_{q,p}\;.\end{split}

(46)

The equations for
\beta^{(1,1)}
and
\beta^{(1,2)}
read analogously.

B.3
F1-model

Since the transition is indicated by the system becoming soft and hence by a vanishing speed of sound, we can neglect the contributions of
w_{q}^{(3)}(s)
. As it was shown, this does neither effect the prediction of Rayleigh-damping nor affects the dispersion relation quantitatively.
\hat{w}_{q}^{(3)}(s)
becomes non-negligible only for higher frequencies. Thus, we can propose a simple self-consistent model, which is valid in the low frequency regime and which is mathematically equivalent to the well studied F1-model
[
48
,
71
]
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{w}_{q}(s)&=\hat{\Delta}_{q}(s)+\hat{w}_{q}^{(2)}(s)\\
&=\hat{\Delta}_{q}(s)+\sum_{p}p^{d-1}C_{q,p}\hat{\chi}_{p}(s)\;.\end{split}

(47)

Since the vertices
\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}
can be calculated from the pair-potential
U(r)
and density correlation functions, this self-consistent model can potentially be solved numerically and compared to experiments. Since it its numerically quite costly to determine the stability matrix, this is postponed to a future work.

Appendix C
Mapping to the field theory of the Euclidean Random matrix model

The euclidean random matrix model introduced by Mezard, Parisi and Zee
[
66
]
has gained a lot of attention in the last 25 years
[
25
,
66
,
67
,
22
,
24
,
26
,
68
,
28
]
. The model is about investigating particles harmonically oscillating around random and fixed positions. Thus, it is a simplistic model for stable topological disorder. The system is specified by the random matrix
\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}}\hskip 1.0pt
which constitutes the potential energy

\displaystyle U=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j}\phi_{i}\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}}\hskip 1.0pt_{ij}\phi_{j}\equiv m\frac{\omega_{0}^{2}}{4}\sum_{i,j}f(\boldsymbol{r}_{i}-\boldsymbol{r}_{j})(\phi_{i}-\phi_{j})^{2}\;.

(48)

Here,
\phi_{i}
denote small fluctuations around the frozen positions
\boldsymbol{r}_{i}
. Note, that the vector character of the displacements is neglected. But still these scalar fluctuations have the dimension of a displacement. It is requested that the
Fourier transformation
f(\boldsymbol{q})
of the unitless spring function exists. Further, we assume rotational invariace, which implies that
f
only depends on the absolute value of the wavevector
q=|\boldsymbol{q}|
. Lastly, we assume that the spring
function is regular. All in all, this implies
f(0)-f(\boldsymbol{q})\propto q^{2}
. Note, that the ERM-model is supposed to describe a Newtonian system. Thus, we set
\xi=0
in this section. All the proposed solutions of the ERM-model, known by the authors, assume a uniform distribution of the particles. This amounts to
S_{q}=1
. Performing a high density expansion, the susceptibility can be written as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\chi}^{ERM}_{q}(s)&=\overline{\sum_{i,j}e^{-i\boldsymbol{q}\cdot(\boldsymbol{r}_{i}-\boldsymbol{r}_{j})}\left[\frac{1}{s^{2}+\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}}\hskip 1.0pt}\right]_{ij}}\\
&=\frac{1}{s^{2}-V_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{q}}^{ERM}+\sigma_{q}^{ERM}(s)}\;.\end{split}

(49)

The supersrcipted
ERM
indicates that the quantities refer to the ERM model. The susceptibility or Green’s function
\hat{\chi}^{ERM}_{q}(s)
is here the resolvent of the random matrix
\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}}\hskip 1.0pt
. The overline means averaging over the disorder,
i.e.
the random positions. A self-consistent Born approximation was proposed in
[
22
]
. The proposed equation for the self-energy reads

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{q}(s)^{SCB,ERM}=-\frac{1}{n}\int\frac{d^{d}\boldsymbol{k}}{(2\pi)^{d}}V^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}\hat{\chi}_{k}^{ERM}(s)V^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}\;.

(50)

Note, that we consider the thermodynamic limit
V,N\to\infty
and
\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\to\frac{V}{(2\pi)^{d}}\int d^{d}\boldsymbol{k}
through out this section, being in accordance with the earlier works on the ERM model.
The model defined in equation (
50
) fails to predict Rayleigh-damping. One finds in the hydrodynamic limit:
\text{Im}\{\sigma_{q}(s=-i\omega+0^{+})^{SCB,ERM}\}\propto\omega^{d-2}q^{2}
. Another self-consistent theory was proposed in
[
25
]
by two of the present authors, which takes non-planar contributions into account. The self-energy reads

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{q}(s)^{VF,ERM}=-\frac{1}{n}\int\frac{d^{d}\boldsymbol{k}}{(2\pi)^{d}}V_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}^{ERM}\hat{\chi}_{k}^{(0)}(s)\hat{\mathcal{U}}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}^{ERM}(s)\;,

(51a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\bigg[1-\frac{1}{n}\int\frac{d^{d}\boldsymbol{p}}{(2\pi)^{d}}V^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p}}\hat{\chi}^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{p}}(s)V^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p}}\hat{\chi}_{k}^{(0)}(s)\bigg]\hat{\mathcal{U}}^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}(s)\\
&\approx V^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}+\frac{1}{n}\int\frac{d^{d}\boldsymbol{p}}{(2\pi)^{d}}V^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p}+\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{q}}\hat{\chi}^{ERM}_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{p}-\boldsymbol{k}|}(s)\\
&\hskip 39.83368pt\times V^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{p},\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{p}-\boldsymbol{k}}\hat{\chi}_{p}^{(0)}(s)\hat{\mathcal{U}}^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{p}}(s)\;.\end{split}

(51b)

with the bare propagator
\chi_{q}^{(0)}(s)=\frac{1}{s^{2}-V_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{q}}^{ERM}}
. This model predicts the correct sound attenuation. There are different models proposed in
[
26
]
. But here, the authors dismantle the vertex
V_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}^{ERM}
in more elementary parts before summing up parts of them again. Since its structure is hence not easily accessible, we leave this work aside here. We will show in the following how the two self-consistent solutions for the self-energy
\hat{\sigma}_{q}(s)^{VF,ERM}
and
\hat{\sigma}_{q}(s)^{SCB,ERM}
arise in an high density or small integrated fluidity approximation.

Hereinafter, we look at the susceptibility
\hat{\chi}_{q}(s)
in the high density limit, were
c_{q}^{\perp}
becomes large. The self-energy (Eq.
10b
) approximately reads

\hat{\sigma}_{q}(s)\approx-q^{2}(c_{q}^{\perp})^{4}\hat{w}_{q}^{\perp}(s)\;.

(52)

We start by defining the scalar ERM-vertex
{V}^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}=\frac{q(c_{q}^{\perp})^{2}}{d-1}{\rm Tr}\{\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}\}
. The vertex vanishes with
aq^{2}+b\boldsymbol{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{k}
for
\boldsymbol{q}\to 0
and has all the properties of the general vertex used in ERM investigations
[
24
,
25
]
. Starting from equation (
72
) and utilising Einsteins sum convention one finds

\displaystyle\begin{split}V_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}^{ERM}=&2\frac{q_{\gamma}(\delta_{\alpha\tau}-\hat{q}_{\alpha}\hat{q}_{\tau})}{mV(d-1)}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\kappa_{\zeta}\text{Re}\{X_{\tau\gamma}(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\boldsymbol{q})\}(\delta_{\alpha\zeta}-\hat{k}_{\alpha}\hat{k}_{\zeta})\\
&\bigg[\frac{\braket{\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{\kappa}-\boldsymbol{k})\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{\kappa}),\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k})}}{NS_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}|}}-S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{\kappa}|}\bigg]\;.\end{split}

(53)

Thus, we can express the high frequency
transverse velocity
c_{q}^{\perp}
via the scalar ERM-vertex

\displaystyle\begin{split}V_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{q}}^{ERM}&=\frac{-2(\delta_{\nu\gamma}-\hat{q}_{\gamma}\hat{q}_{\nu})}{(d-1)Vm}q_{\mu}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\kappa_{\nu}\text{Re}\{X_{\mu\gamma}(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\boldsymbol{q})\}S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{\kappa}|}\\
&=-(qc_{q}^{\perp})^{2}\;.\end{split}

(54)

This holds due to the fact of

\delta\rho(0)=0
. As a consequence, we recover the bare propagator of the ERM-theory
\chi_{q}^{(0)}(s)=\Big[s^{2}-V_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{q}}^{ERM}\Big]^{-1}
. To relate our theory to the Euclidean Random Matrix model, we express the vertex as well with a structure independent scalar and isotropic function

\displaystyle V^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}\longrightarrow n^{*}\omega_{0}^{2}\frac{f(|\boldsymbol{k}|)-f(|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}|)}{f(q=0)}\;.

(55)

Similarly to the bare vertex, we set
q(c_{q}^{\perp})^{2}\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{U}}}\hskip 1.0pt}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}\to\hat{\chi}_{k}^{(0),\perp}(s)S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}|}\hat{\mathcal{U}}^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}(\mathbb{1}-\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}})
. Here, we also
pulled
out a bare propagator and a static structure factor of the renormalised vertex. This allows the diagrammatic representation given in the main text. See equation (
23
). The associated Feynman rules read:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hbox to26.32pt{\vbox to14.99pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.06697pt\lower-1.06697pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}

}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{10.09251pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 13.97 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.77 0 L -1.66 2.21 L 0 0 L -1.66 -2.21}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{}{}{{}}

{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 3.94 0 C 3.94 2.17 2.17 3.94 0 3.94 C -2.17 3.94 -3.94 2.17 -3.94 0 C -3.94 -2.17 -2.17 -3.94 0 -3.94 C 2.17 -3.94 3.94 -2.17 3.94 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
