{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray={3.0pt,3.0pt},stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 33.46 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\equiv 1\;.\end{split}

(56)

The self-energy is constituted by all irreducible diagrams. Drawing them, one has to keep in mind that the
momentum is conserved at every vertex. One has to integrate over the internal momenta.

Multiplying equation (
26
) with the renormalized vertex and integrating over
\boldsymbol{p}
leads to

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int\frac{d^{d}\boldsymbol{p}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\hskip 1.0pt}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p},s)&\cdot(\mathbb{1}-\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}})\chi_{p}^{(0)}(s)S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{p}|}\hat{\mathcal{U}}^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{p}}(s)=\frac{1}{s}k^{2}(c^{\perp}_{k})^{2}\chi_{k^{\prime}}^{(0)}(s)S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}|}\;(\mathbb{1}-\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}})\;\hat{\mathcal{U}}^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}(s)\\
&-\frac{1}{n}\int\frac{d^{d}\boldsymbol{p}d^{d}\boldsymbol{b}}{(2\pi)^{2d}}{V}^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{b}}\hat{\chi}_{\boldsymbol{b}}(s)S_{|\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{b}|}{V}^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{b}}\frac{\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\hskip 1.0pt}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p},s)\chi_{p}^{(0)}(s)S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{p}|}\cdot(\mathbb{1}-\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}})\;\hat{\mathcal{U}}^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{p}}(s)}{k^{2}(c_{k}^{\perp})^{2}}\\
&-\frac{1}{n}\int\frac{d^{d}\boldsymbol{p}d^{d}\boldsymbol{b}}{(2\pi)^{2d}}{V}^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{b}+\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{q}}\hat{\chi}_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{b}-\boldsymbol{k}|}(s)S_{\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{b}}{V}^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{p},\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{b}-\boldsymbol{k}}\frac{\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\hskip 1.0pt}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{p},s)\chi_{p}^{(0)}(s)S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{p}|}\cdot(\mathbb{1}-\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}})\;\hat{\mathcal{U}}^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{p}}(s)}{p^{2}(c_{p}^{\perp})^{2}}\;.\end{split}

(57)

Since the numerators of the integral kernels in the lines two and three of equation (
57
) vanish at least with
k^{d+1}
and
p^{d+1}
, the small wavevector contributions to the integral are negligible. Also, the contribution of the sound pole
-s^{2}=p^{2}(c_{p}^{\perp})^{2}
in the third and forth line becomes small in the considered limit. The contribution to the damping is in accordance with Rayleigh-damping, as shown in section
B.2
. However, the contribution becomes small, due to the factor
1/(c_{q}^{\perp})^{2}
. Thus, the first term dominates for
c^{\perp}_{k}\to\infty
and we find in this high density approximation

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int\frac{d^{d}\boldsymbol{p}}{(2\pi)^{d}}&\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\hskip 1.0pt}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p},s)\cdot(\mathbb{1}-\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}})\chi_{p}^{(0)}(s)S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{p}|}\hat{\mathcal{U}}^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}(s)\\
&\approx\frac{1}{s}k^{2}(c^{\perp}_{k})^{2}\chi_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{(0)}(s)S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}|}(\mathbb{1}-\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}})\hat{\mathcal{U}}^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}(s)\;.\end{split}

(58)

Having this, we arrive at a concise equation for the scalar renormalised ERM vertex

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\hat{\mathcal{U}}^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}(s)\approx{V}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}+\frac{1}{n}\int\frac{d^{d}\boldsymbol{p}}{(2\pi)^{d}}{V}^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p}}\hat{\chi}_{\boldsymbol{p}}(s)S_{|\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{p}|}{V}^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p}}\hat{\chi}_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{(0)}(s)V^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}(s)\\
&+\frac{1}{n}\int\frac{d^{d}\boldsymbol{p}}{(2\pi)^{d}}{V}^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{k},-\boldsymbol{q}+\boldsymbol{p}+\boldsymbol{k}}\hat{\chi}_{|-\boldsymbol{q}+\boldsymbol{p}+\boldsymbol{k}|}(s)S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{p}|}{V}^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{p},-\boldsymbol{q}+\boldsymbol{p}+\boldsymbol{k}}\hat{\chi}_{\boldsymbol{p}}^{(0)}(s)V^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{p}}(s)\;.\end{split}

(59)

This equation is diagrammatically represented in the main text in equation
(
23
).
In the last line we can approximate
\frac{1}{k^{2}(pc_{p}^{\perp})^{2}}\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{p}}\approx\hat{\chi}_{p}^{(0),ERM}\hat{\mathcal{U}}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{p}}^{ERM}(s)
. This becomes an exact approximation deep in the jammed state for small frequencies. Looking at the diagrammatic representation (
23
), this approximation means adding more loop-diagrams. Since every new loop comes with a factor
\frac{1}{n}
this does neither quantitatively nor qualitatively change the high density state. The contributions to the sound attenuation of the
erroneously
introduced sound pole vanishes faster that the Rayleigh sound attenuation for
s,q\to 0.
Meaning, the new pole only leads to higher corrections of the sound attenuation for
s,q\to 0
.
Thus, we find for the susceptibility of the transverse modes deep in the jammed state

\displaystyle\hat{\chi}_{q}(s)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{s(s+\xi)+q^{2}(c_{q}^{\perp})^{2}+\sigma^{ERM}_{q}(s)}\;,

(60a)

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}^{ERM}_{q}(s)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{n}\int\frac{d^{d}\boldsymbol{k}}{(2\pi)^{d}}V_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}^{ERM}\hat{\chi}_{k}^{(0)}S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}|}\hat{\mathcal{U}}^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}(s)\;.

(60b)

with the diagrammatic representation of the self energy

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\sigma}^{ERM}_{q}(s)\approx&\;\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to34.83pt{\vbox to22.85pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.84544pt\lower-3.533pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} {}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 35.04 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{10.66148pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 14.75 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.77 0 L -1.66 2.21 L 0 0 L -1.66 -2.21}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}}
}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}}

{{}{}{}{}}
{{}{}{}{}}
}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{}{}{}}
{{}{}{}{}}
}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{}{}{}}
{{}{}{}{}}
}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} {}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.97 37.01 C 3.7 37.01 4.56 40.47 6.29 40.47 C 7.54 40.33 8.43 38.54 9.36 36.66 C 10.3 34.78 11.19 32.99 12.44 32.85 C 13.64 32.51 15.14 33.83 16.72 35.22 C 18.29 36.61 19.79 37.94 21 37.59 C 22.12 37.05 22.35 35.06 22.59 32.97 C 22.83 30.88 23.06 28.89 24.19 28.35 C 25.2 27.61 27.07 28.35 29.02 29.13 C 30.97 29.9 32.83 30.64 33.85 29.91 C 34.72 29.01 34.27 27.06 33.79 25.01 C 33.31 22.97 32.86 21.02 33.74 20.12 C 34.45 19.09 36.45 19.16 38.55 19.23 C 40.65 19.31 42.65 19.38 43.36 18.34 C 43.88 17.21 42.8 15.52 41.66 13.76 C 40.52 11.99 39.43 10.31 39.95 9.17 L 42.13 -3.15}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\end{split}

(61)

These equations are the same as the ones derived in
[
25
]
for the renormalized vertex in the ERM-model:
\sigma^{ERM}_{q}(s)=\sigma^{VF,ERM}_{q}(s)
. The same set of irreducible diagrams survives in the high density regime. One has to
neglect excluded volume effects and choose a uniform distribution of the particles to fully recover the self-consistent model in
[
25
]
. This results in the approximation
S_{q}\approx 1.

All the numerical results shown in the main text were obtained by neglecting the tensor structure of the vertices

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(c_{q}^{\perp})^{2}\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{V}}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}\to\hat{\chi}_{k}^{(0),\perp}(s)S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}|}\mathcal{V}^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}(\mathbb{1}-\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}})\;,\\
&{V}^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}=\frac{q(c_{q}^{\perp})^{2}}{d-1}{\rm Tr}\{\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}\}\equiv\frac{n^{*}\omega_{0}^{2}f(|\boldsymbol{k}|)-f(|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}|)}{f(q=0)-f(q=2\pi/\sigma)}\;,\end{split}

(62)

but by explicitly not performing the high density expansion discussed above.
Neglecting excluded volume effects and setting
S_{q}=1
leads to the following self-consistent set of equations

\displaystyle\hat{K}_{q}^{ERM}(s)=

\displaystyle\left[s+\xi-\frac{V^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{q}}}{s-\frac{1}{V^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{q}}}\hat{W}_{q}^{ERM}(s)}\right]^{-1}\;

(63a)

\displaystyle\hat{W}_{q}^{ERM}(s)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\int\frac{d^{d}\boldsymbol{k}}{(2\pi)^{d}}{V}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}^{ERM}\hat{\chi}_{k}^{(0)}(s){\mathcal{V}}^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}(s)\;,

(63b)

\displaystyle\hat{\Xi}^{ERM}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p},s)

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p}}}{n}\int\frac{d^{d}\boldsymbol{b}}{(2\pi)^{d}}V^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{b}}\hat{K}^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{b}}(s)V^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{b}}+\frac{1}{n}V^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p}+\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{q}}\hat{K}^{ERM}_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{p}-\boldsymbol{k}|}(s)V^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{p},-\boldsymbol{q}+\boldsymbol{p}+\boldsymbol{k}}

(63c)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int\frac{d^{d}\boldsymbol{p}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\bigg[\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p}}-(s+\xi)&\;\hat{\Xi}^{ERM}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p},s)\chi^{(0)}_{p}(s)\bigg]\hat{\mathcal{V}}^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{p}}(s)=\int\frac{d^{d}\boldsymbol{p}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\Big[s\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p}}+\hat{\Xi}^{ERM}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p},s)\hat{\chi}_{p}^{(0)}(0)\bigg]V^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{p}}\;.\end{split}

(63d)

We approximate the left hand side to be equal to the renormalised vertex for
s\to 0
. This basically amounts to an
F1
approximation. Note, that this approximation is exact for
s=0
in the jammed state. We extend its validity to the small frequency regime of the un-jammed phase, in order to test our equations in this phase as well. One arrives at

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\hat{\mathcal{V}}^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}(s)\\
&\underset{s\to 0}{\approx}\int\frac{d^{d}\boldsymbol{p}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\Big[s\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p}}+\hat{\Xi}^{ERM}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p},s)\chi_{p}^{(0)}(s)\bigg]V^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{p}}\;.\end{split}

(64)

All numerical results presented in this work are derived from the equations (
63
) and (
64
).

Appendix D
Un-jammed phase

In the un-jammed state decorrelate stresses with time and decay for
t\to\infty
. This causes a finite value of

\displaystyle\kappa_{q}^{\perp}=\lim_{s\to 0}(s+\xi)\hat{K}_{q}^{\perp}(s)\;.

(65)

In the Newtonian case can
\kappa^{\perp}_{q}
be interpreted as a non-ergodicity parameter. As a consequence,
(s+\xi)\hat{W}_{q}(s)\equiv\hat{\mathcal{W}}_{q}(s)
and
(s+\xi)\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{V}}}\hskip 1.0pt}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}(s)\equiv\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}}\hskip 1.0pt}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}(s)
also yield finite contributions for
s\to 0
. Writing
\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}}\hskip 1.0pt}_{k}(s)=(s+\xi)\hat{M}_{k}(s)(\mathbb{1}-\hat{\boldsymbol{k}}\hat{\boldsymbol{k}})
, equation (
32
) reads after multiplying with
(s+\xi)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left[s(s+\xi)+k^{2}(c_{k}^{\perp})^{2}+(c_{k}^{\perp})^{2}\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}}\hskip 1.0pt}_{k}(s)\right]\cdot\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}}\hskip 1.0pt}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}(s)+(s+\xi)\;\frac{(c_{k}^{\perp})^{2}}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{p}}\underline{\boldsymbol{\tilde{V}}}\hskip 1.0pt(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p})\cdot\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}}\hskip 1.0pt}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{p}}(s)\;\hat{K}_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{p}|}(s)=\\
=&\left[s(s+\xi)+(c_{k}^{\perp})^{2}\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}}\hskip 1.0pt}_{k}(s)\right]\cdot\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}^{\dagger}S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}|}+(s+\xi)\frac{(c_{k}^{\perp})^{2}}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{p}}\underline{\boldsymbol{\tilde{V}}}\hskip 1.0pt(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p})\cdot\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{p},\boldsymbol{q}}^{\dagger}\hat{K}_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{p}|}(s)S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{p}|}\;.\end{split}

(66)

The set of equations does not simplify much in the
s\to 0
limit. One finds

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sum_{\boldsymbol{p}}\bigg\{\left[k^{2}+\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}}\hskip 1.0pt}_{k}(s)\right]\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p}}+\underline{\boldsymbol{\tilde{V}}}\hskip 1.0pt(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p})\frac{\kappa^{\perp}_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{p}|}}{N}\bigg\}\cdot\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}}\hskip 1.0pt}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{p}}(s)\\
=&\sum_{\boldsymbol{p}}\left\{\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}}\hskip 1.0pt}_{p}(s)\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p}}+\frac{1}{N}\underline{\boldsymbol{\tilde{V}}}\hskip 1.0pt(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p})\;\kappa^{\perp}_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{p}|}\right\}\cdot\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{p},\boldsymbol{q}}^{\dagger}S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{p}|}\;.\end{split}

(67)

However,
\lim_{s\to 0}(s+\xi)\hat{K}_{q}^{\perp}(s)
is suppose to become small, when approaching the jamming transition. Since we are mainly interested in this case, we can neglect higher orders in
\kappa^{\perp}_{q}
. This leads to the this simplifies to

\displaystyle\begin{split}k^{2}\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}}\hskip 1.0pt}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}(s)\approx&\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}}\hskip 1.0pt}_{k}(s=0)\cdot\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}^{\dagger}\;S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}|}\\
&+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{p}}\underline{\boldsymbol{\tilde{V}}}\hskip 1.0pt(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p})\cdot\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{p},\boldsymbol{q}}^{\dagger}\;\kappa^{\perp}_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{p}|}\;\;.\end{split}

(68)

Again, we end up with a simple expression for the
s=0
limit

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{W}}_{q}(s=0)=\sum_{p}p^{d-1}C_{q,p}\kappa_{p}

(69)

Looking at the long time limit,
\hat{K}^{\perp}_{q}(t\to\infty)=\lim_{s\to 0}s\hat{K}_{q}^{\perp}(s),
one finds in the Langevin case

\displaystyle\lim_{s\to 0}s\hat{K}_{q}^{\perp}(s)=\frac{s}{\xi}\frac{1}{1+\frac{q^{2}}{\mathcal{W}_{q}(s=0)}}\;.

(70)

Appendix E
Vertex evaluation

In this section do we analyse the vertex

\displaystyle{V}_{\alpha\beta}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})=-\frac{\braket{F^{\perp}_{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{q})\Omega Q\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{q})v^{\perp}_{\beta}(-\boldsymbol{k})}}{Nk_{B}Tq(c_{q}^{\perp})^{2}S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}|}}\;,

(71)

explicitly. We are going to express
{V}_{\alpha\beta}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})
in terms of density correlation functions. Hence,
{V}_{\alpha\beta}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})
can be calculated for real systems.
We use Einstein’s sum convention in this section.
We end up with

\displaystyle\begin{split}-\frac{\braket{F^{\perp}_{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{q})\Omega Q\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k})v^{\perp}_{\beta}(-\boldsymbol{k}}}{Nq(c_{q}^{\perp})^{2}k_{B}TS_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}|}}=&2\frac{q_{\gamma}}{mVq(c_{q}^{\perp})^{2}}(\delta_{\alpha\tau}-\hat{q}_{\alpha}\hat{q}_{\tau})\sum_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\kappa_{\zeta}(\delta_{\beta\zeta}-\hat{k}_{\beta}\hat{k}_{\zeta})\text{Re}\{X_{\tau\gamma}(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\boldsymbol{q})\}\\
&\times\bigg[\frac{\braket{\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{\kappa}-\boldsymbol{k})\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{\kappa})\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k})}}{NS_{|\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{q}|}}-S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{\kappa}|}\bigg]\end{split}

(72)

Applying the convolution approximation
\frac{1}{N}\braket{\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{k})\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}),\delta\rho(-\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})}\approx S_{k}S_{k^{\prime}}S_{|\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}|}

[
39
]
for the three-point correlator, gives the concise formulae

\displaystyle\begin{split}-&\frac{\braket{F^{\perp}_{\alpha}(\boldsymbol{q})\Omega\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k})v^{\perp}_{\beta}(-\boldsymbol{k}}}{Nq(c_{q}^{\perp})^{2}k_{B}TS_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}|}}\approx-2\frac{q_{\gamma}(\delta_{\alpha\tau}-\hat{q}_{\alpha}\hat{q}_{\tau})}{mVq(c_{q}^{\perp})^{2}}\\
&\times\sum_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\kappa_{\zeta}(\delta_{\beta\zeta}-\hat{k}_{\beta}\hat{k}_{\zeta})\text{Re}\{X_{\tau\gamma}(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\boldsymbol{q})\}S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{\kappa}|}\Big[1-S_{|\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{\kappa}|}\Big]\end{split}

(73)

The weights are determined by the interaction potential

\displaystyle X_{\alpha\beta}(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\boldsymbol{q})=-\frac{1}{2}\int d\boldsymbol{r}e^{i\boldsymbol{\kappa}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}}\frac{r_{\alpha}r_{\beta}}{r}U^{\prime}(r)\frac{1-e^{-i\boldsymbol{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}}}{i\boldsymbol{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}}\;,

(74)

Note, that the vertex depends on the density and the local structure.

E.1
Symmetry of the Vertex

It is easy to see, that
\text{Re}[X_{\alpha\beta}(-\boldsymbol{k},-\boldsymbol{q})]=\text{Re}[X_{\alpha\beta}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})]
holds.
Additionally, the correlation function of scalar quantities only depends on the absolute modulus of the wavevector
[
48
]
.
After a change of the integration variable, we hence find

\displaystyle\begin{split}V_{\alpha\beta}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k})&=V_{\alpha\beta}(-\boldsymbol{q},-\boldsymbol{k})=V_{\beta\alpha}^{\dagger}(\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q})\;,\\
\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{q},0}&=\textrm{{{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}-}}}\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{q},0}\;,\hskip 19.91684pt\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{0,\boldsymbol{k}}=-\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{0,-\boldsymbol{k}}\end{split}

(75)

E.2
Hydrodynamic limit

The weights are a symmetric function of
\boldsymbol{\kappa}
in
\boldsymbol{q}=0
,
i.e

\underline{\boldsymbol{X}}\hskip 1.0pt(\boldsymbol{\kappa},0)=\underline{\boldsymbol{X}}\hskip 1.0pt(-\boldsymbol{\kappa},0)
. But the whole argument
\kappa_{\zeta}\text{Re}\{X_{\tau\gamma}(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\boldsymbol{0})\}\ \bigg[\frac{\braket{\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{\kappa})\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{\kappa}),\delta\rho(\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k})}}{NS_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}|}}-S_{\kappa}\bigg]
is an odd-function of
\boldsymbol{\kappa}
,
since the average over scalar quantities does only depend on the absolutest modulus of the wavevector. Thus, the zeroth order in
\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}
of the integral over the three-point function vanishes. Since the integrand stays non-zero for only
\boldsymbol{k}\to 0
or
\boldsymbol{q}\to 0
, we find in the hydrodynamic limit.

\displaystyle V_{\alpha\beta}(\boldsymbol{k}\to 0,\boldsymbol{q}\to 0)=V^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta\gamma\eta}(0,0)\frac{q_{\gamma}q_{\eta}}{q}+V^{(2)}_{\alpha\beta\gamma\eta}(0,0)\frac{q_{\gamma}k_{\eta}}{q}

(76)

In the case of the ERM-approximation, (see chapter
C
) or when approximating the vertex to have a diagonal form, one finds

\displaystyle\begin{split}V^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}&=\frac{q(c_{q}^{\perp})^{2}}{d-1}\text{Tr}\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}\;\\
V^{ERM}_{\boldsymbol{k}\to 0,\boldsymbol{q}\to 0}&=V^{(1)}q^{2}+V^{(2)}_{\alpha\beta}q_{\alpha}k_{\beta}\\
&=V^{(1)}q+V^{(2)}\boldsymbol{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{k}\end{split}

(77)

The last equality holds to the assumed isotropy of the system, which implies
V^{(2)}_{\alpha\beta}\propto\delta_{\alpha\beta}
.

Appendix F
Götze-scaling analysis

The dispersion relation
v_{q}^{\perp}
and the non-ergodicity parameter
\kappa_{q}^{\perp}
both vanish,when approaching the (un-) jamming transition, or respectively near the critical density
n_{c}
where the maximal eigenvalue of the stability matrix becomes
1
. Utilising that the dynamics close to the transition is governed by the eigenvector of the stability matrix
C_{q,p}
belonging to the largest eigenvalue, we derive the scaling of important quantities like
v_{q}^{\perp},\kappa_{q}^{\perp}
close to
n_{c}
. Basically, our analysis involves standard linear algebra (Fredholm’s theorem ) and is based on the assumption that the largest eigenvalue is simple.

F.1
The scaling equation

We start by re-writing equation (
32
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\underline{\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}(s)=-(c_{k}^{\perp})^{2}\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}}\hskip 1.0pt_{k}(s)\cdot\underline{\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}(s)-(c_{k}^{\perp})^{2}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{p}}\underline{\boldsymbol{\tilde{V}}}\hskip 1.0pt(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p})\cdot\underline{\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{p}}(s)\;\overline{K}_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{p}|}(s)\\
=&\left[s(s+\xi)+(c_{k}^{\perp})^{2}\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}}\hskip 1.0pt_{k}(s)\right]\cdot\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}+(c_{k}^{\perp})^{2}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{p}}\underline{\boldsymbol{\tilde{V}}}\hskip 1.0pt(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p})\cdot\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{p},\boldsymbol{p}}^{\dagger}\;\overline{K}_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{p}|}(s)\;\;.\end{split}

(78)

Here, we have introduced for the purpose of readability
(s+\xi)\hat{K}_{q}^{\perp}(s)=\overline{K}^{\perp}_{q}(s)
and
\boldsymbol{b}=\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{p}-\boldsymbol{k}
. Further, we wrote again
(s+\xi)\hat{M}_{k}(s)(\mathbb{1}-\hat{\boldsymbol{k}}\hat{\boldsymbol{k}})=\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}}\hskip 1.0pt}_{k}(s)
.
The conceptual idea is that the quantity

\displaystyle s\varphi_{q}(s)=\frac{s(s+\xi)}{(c_{q}^{\perp})^{2}}+(s+\xi)\hat{W}^{\perp}_{q}(s)

(79)

becomes small for
s\to 0
when approaching the transition. Note, that the shear modulus can be expressed with this quantity as
G_{q}^{\perp}(s)=\frac{s+\xi}{s\varphi_{q}(s)}
. In the un-jammed phase holds
\lim_{s,\boldsymbol{q}\to 0}s\varphi_{q}(s)=\frac{1}{(\lambda_{-})^{2}}
. In the jammed phase holds
\lim_{s\to 0}s\varphi_{q}(s)=\frac{s(s+\xi)}{(v_{q}^{\perp})^{2}}
. We can express the transverse velocity autocorrelation as

\displaystyle\overline{K}_{q}(s)=\frac{s\varphi_{q}(s)}{s\varphi_{q}(s)+q^{2}}=\frac{s\varphi_{q}(s)}{q^{2}}-\frac{(s\varphi_{q}(s))^{2}}{s\varphi_{q}(s)+q^{2}}\frac{1}{q^{2}}\;.

(80)

In order to deal with our set of self-consistent equations, we expand the renormalized vertex on the right hand side of equation (
78
) according to

\displaystyle\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}}\hskip 1.0pt}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{p}}(s)=\frac{s(s+\xi)}{s(s+\xi)+(pc_{p}^{\perp})^{2}}S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{p}|}\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{p},\boldsymbol{q}}^{\dagger}\;.

(81)

This ansatz leads to

\displaystyle\begin{split}s\varphi_{q}(s)-&\frac{s(s+\xi)}{(c_{q}^{\perp})^{2}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}:\hat{\underline{\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}}\hskip 1.0pt}_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}(s)\\
=&-\frac{s(s+\xi)}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p}}\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}:\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{q}}^{\dagger}\frac{(c_{k}^{\perp})^{2}s\varphi_{p}(s)S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{k}|}}{[s\varphi_{p}(s)+p^{2}][s(s+\xi)+k^{2}(c_{k}^{\perp})^{2}]}\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p}}:\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{p},\boldsymbol{k}}^{\dagger}S_{|\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{p}|}\\
&-\frac{s(s+\xi)}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p}}\frac{(c_{k}^{\perp})^{2}s\varphi_{b}(s)S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{p}|}}{[s\varphi_{b}(s)+b^{2}][s(s+\xi)+p^{2}(c_{p}^{\perp})^{2}]}\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}:(\tilde{\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p})\cdot\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{p},\boldsymbol{q}}^{\dagger})\\
&+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k}}:\left\{\Big[s(s+\xi)+(c_{k}^{\perp})^{2}\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{M}}}\hskip 1.0pt_{k}(s)\Big]\cdot\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p}}^{\dagger}S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{p}|}+\frac{(c_{k}^{\perp})^{2}}{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{p}}\tilde{\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{p})\cdot\underline{\boldsymbol{V}}\hskip 1.0pt_{\boldsymbol{p},\boldsymbol{q}}^{\dagger}\;\overline{K}_{b}(s)\right\}S_{|\boldsymbol{q}-\boldsymbol{p}|}\;,\end{split}

(82)

Since the terms in the second and third line are proportional to
s(s+\xi)
, they can be neglected for small frequencies. The term
s(s+\xi)
in the fourth line has to be kept, since it plays a role in the jammed phase. Relying on the decomposition of
\overline{K}
, we arrive at

\displaystyle\begin{split}s\varphi_{q}(s)&-\frac{s(s+\xi)}{(\tilde{c}_{q}^{\perp}(s))^{2}}-\sum_{p}p^{d-3}C_{q,p}s\varphi_{p}(s)\\
&=-\sum_{p}p^{d-3}C_{q,p}\frac{(s\varphi_{p}(s))^{2}}{s\varphi_{p}(s)+p^{2}}\;.\end{split}

(83)

In the small
s
limit can we set
\tilde{c}_{q}^{\perp}(s)\approx\tilde{c}_{q}^{\perp}(0)\equiv\tilde{c}^{\perp}_{q}
. Its contribution of the sound pole becomes subdominant close to the transition.

One has to notice a few things here

•

Close to the transition vanishes
s\varphi_{p}(s)
in the small frequency limit. Hence, this term can be set to zero in the denominators on the right hand side, since it will eventually be smaller than
p^{2}
for any wavenumber
p

•

Note, that the term on the right hand side of equation (
83
) features a potential divergence for
p\to 0
close to the transition, where
s\varphi_{p}(s)\approx 0
can be set to zero in the denominator
[
72
]
. At least in in lower dimensions
d<5
. But it turns out, that this is not a problem, since the memory function
\hat{W}^{\perp}_{q}(s)
close to the transition is determined by the left and right eigenvector
\hat{h}_{p}
and
h_{q}
of the stability matrix associated with its largest eigenvector. As it will be shown in the next subsection, the critical left eigenvector vanishes
\hat{h}_{p}\propto p^{2}
for
p\to 0
. This prevents the divergence.

We interpret
\underline{\boldsymbol{C}}\hskip 1.0pt\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
, where
m\to\infty
is the number of wavevectors considered. Hence,
\underline{\boldsymbol{C}}\hskip 1.0pt
as a square matrix can expressed via its Jordanform

\displaystyle\underline{\boldsymbol{C}}\hskip 1.0pt=\underline{\boldsymbol{E}}\hskip 1.0pt^{-1}\underline{\boldsymbol{J}}\hskip 1.0pt\underline{\boldsymbol{E}}\hskip 1.0pt\;,

(84)

where
\underline{\boldsymbol{J}}\hskip 1.0pt
is the Jordan block-matrix.
\underline{\boldsymbol{E}}\hskip 1.0pt
is constituted by the generalised eigenvectors of
\underline{\boldsymbol{C}}\hskip 1.0pt
. One has to remember that, if
(\boldsymbol{v}^{(i)})^{T}
is a left eigenvector to the eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}
and
\boldsymbol{u}^{(j)}
is a right generalised eigenvector to
\lambda_{j}\neq\lambda_{i}
then
(\boldsymbol{v}^{(i)})^{T}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}^{(j)}=0
holds.

We assume, that the largest eigenvalue of
\underline{\boldsymbol{C}}\hskip 1.0pt
is simple,
i.e
its geometric multiplicity is 1. In this case can we order the eigenvalues and set

\displaystyle J_{1,1}=\lambda_{max}=1+\epsilon\;,\hskip 28.45274pt\left(\underline{\boldsymbol{E}}\hskip 1.0pt^{-1}\right)_{j,i}=\hat{h}_{j}\;,\;\;E_{1,j}=h_{j}\,.

(85)

As mentioned earlier, the critical eigenvalue of the stability matrix is
\lambda_{max}=1
. We analyse the dynamics close to the transition by setting
\lambda_{max}=1+\epsilon
with
|\epsilon|
small. The spectrum is determined by the density or to be more precise by density correlations functions. We adopt the normalisation
\sum_{q}\hat{h}_{q}h_{q}=1

Assuming the largest eigenvalue being simple, gives

\displaystyle C_{q,p}=(1+\epsilon)h_{q}\hat{h}_{p}+C^{\#}_{q,p}\;,

(86)

with
\sum_{p}C^{\#}_{q,p}h_{p}=0
and
\sum_{q}\hat{h}_{q}C^{\#}_{q,p}=0
.
Again,
s\varphi_{q}(s)
is mainly determined by the critical eigenvector. Hence, we make the ansatz

\displaystyle s\varphi_{q}(s)=\sigma s_{*}g(s_{*})h_{q}+\sigma^{\frac{d}{2}}s_{*}X_{q}^{\#}(s_{*})

(87)

with
s_{*}=st_{*}
, where
\sigma
and
t_{*}=1/\omega_{*}
are yet unspecified.
t_{*}
sets the time scale and
\sigma
is a measure for the distance to the critical point. The orthogonality of the generalised eigenvectors gives
\hat{\boldsymbol{h}}^{T}\cdot\boldsymbol{X}^{\#}=0
. Inserting this ansatz (
87
) in equation (
83
) gives for
s\to 0

\displaystyle\begin{split}-\frac{s(s+\xi)}{(\tilde{c}^{\perp}_{q})^{2}}-&\sigma\epsilon s_{*}g(s_{*})h_{q}+(s_{*}g(s_{*}))^{2}\sigma^{2}\sum_{p}(1+\epsilon)h_{q}\hat{h}_{p}\frac{h_{p}^{2}}{p^{2}}\\
\approx&-\sigma^{\frac{d}{2}}\sum_{p}\Big(\delta_{q,p}-C^{\#}{q,p}\Big)s_{*}X_{p}^{\#}\\
&-\sum_{p}C^{\#}_{q,p}\frac{\sigma^{2}(s_{*}g(s_{*}))^{2}h_{p}^{2}}{p^{2}+s\varphi_{p}(s)}+\mathcal{O}(\sigma^{2.5})\;.\end{split}

(88)

Contracting from the left side with
\hat{\boldsymbol{h}}
together with the adopted normalisation

\displaystyle\sum_{p}\hat{h}_{p}\frac{h_{p}^{2}}{p^{2}}=1\;,

(89)

leads to the scaling equation

\displaystyle\frac{t_{*}^{2}s(s+\xi)}{\sigma^{2}}+\frac{\epsilon}{\sigma}s_{*}g(s_{*})=(1-\epsilon)(s_{*}g(s_{*}))^{2}\underset{\epsilon\to 0}{\longrightarrow}(s_{*}g(s_{*}))^{2}

(90)

with the time-scale parameter resulting from the integral over the inhomogeneous part

\displaystyle\begin{split}t_{*}^{2}&=\int_{0}^{\infty}dq\frac{\hat{h}_{q}}{(\tilde{c}_{q}^{\perp})^{2}}\;.\end{split}

(91)

Additionally, we find the important result

\displaystyle\sigma=|\epsilon|.

(92)

The minus sign corresponds to the jammed phase.

F.2
The critical left eigenvector

As it was explained in the previous section, it is crucial in lower dimensions that the left critical eigenvector vanishes with
p^{2}
. We will argue in this section that this is indeed the case. First, we give a intuitive physical argument. Secondly we will provide a mathematical proof in
d=3
and lastly, we will show numerical results to confirm this prediction.

The physical picture

The matrix
\underline{\boldsymbol{C}}\hskip 1.0pt
has a finite contribution for
p=0
. However, this contribution is of higher order in
q
. More specifically,
C_{q\to 0,0}\propto q^{2}
holds. The un-jamming transition can be understood as the system becoming macroscopically soft. The ability to sustain shear stress ceases and an applied perturbation does not lead to propagating sound waves anymore in the thermodynamic limit but causes macroscopic plastic deformations even for a vanishing amplitude of the perturbation. Nevertheless, the emergence of stability in disordered materials is not a small wavevector phenomenon since the macroscopic stability supervenes on the microscopic structure, which is resolved by higher wavenumbers. Approaching the un-jamming transition from above, more and larger subregions of the system become weakly connected. The restoring forces vanish here. If the number or size of those weakly connected parts becomes extensive, the system becomes macroscopically soft. Thus, the low wavenumber columns and not the rows of the stability matrix
C_{q,p}
are crucial to investigate the un-jamming transition. This is also why the critical part of the stability matrix
C_{q,p}
close to the transition is
p
-dependent but not
q
-dependent. This is confirmed by the observation that the maximal eigenvalue of
C_{q,p}
decreases when the maximal value of the array
p=\{p_{1},p_{2}....p_{m}\}
is chosen too small.

As a consequence, the low
q
-limit of the stability matrix should determine the rigidity of the system. Since this part vanishes with
C_{0,p\to 0}\propto p^{2}
, no divergence should affect the critical dynamics of the dispersion relation close to the un-jamming transition for
d>2
. This, together with the assumed rotational invariance, suggests that the critical left eigenvector vanishes at least with
\hat{h}_{p}\propto p^{2}
in
d=3
.

Mathematical proof

If the entries of the left eigenvector
\hat{h}_{p}
became a constant for
p\to 0
in
d=3
, the sound attenuation would scale with
sq^{2}
for
s,q\to 0
. However, this is in contradiction to our result in section
B.2
:

We consider the case
d=3.

If
\hat{h}_{q}=const.
for
q\to 0
, the normalisation (
89
) would diverge. In this case we couldn’t set
s\varphi_{p}(s)=0
in the denominator. Rather, we had to follow
[
72
]
and adopt the normalisation

\displaystyle 1=\frac{\pi}{2}\sum_{q}\hat{h}_{q}C_{q,0}h_{0}^{3/2}\;.

(93)

The new scaling function would read for
\xi=0

\displaystyle\frac{t_{*}^{2}s^{2}}{\sigma^{3/2}}+\frac{\epsilon}{\sqrt{\sigma}}s_{*}g(s_{*})=(s_{*}g(s_{*}))^{3/2}\;.

(94)

The solution in the jammed state for
s_{*}\ll\frac{\sigma^{3/4}}{t_{*}}
becomes in this case
g_{-}\left(s_{*}\ll\frac{\sigma^{3/4}}{t_{*}}\right)\to s_{*}-s_{*}^{2}
. Since the right eigenvector must stay finite for
q\to 0
, in order for the speed of sound to be finite, the residual contribution to the self-energy becomes

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{q}(s)-\sigma_{q}(0)\propto sq^{2}\;,

(95)

in contradiction to the result obtained in
B.2
. Thus, the left eigenvector must vanish at least with
\hat{h}_{p}\propto p^{2}
in
d=3
.

Numerical confirmation

Figure
5
shows the critical left eigenvector divided by
q^{2}
. One clearly sees, that
\hat{h}_{q}\propto q^{2}
for small wavevectors as predicted by theoretical and physical considerations.

FIG. 5:
The critical left eigenvector divided by
q^{2}
for three different spring functions. A linear Yukawa function
f(r)=\frac{\sigma}{r}\Theta(\sigma-r)
, the Theta-or step-spring function
f(r)=\Theta(\sigma-r)
and the Gaussian spring function
f(r)=e^{-r^{2}/(2\sigma^{2})}
. the left eigenvector is shown with a minimal wavevector
q_{min}\sigma=0.34
with a linear distribution of the wavevectors and with
q_{min}\sigma=0.3
with a logarithmic distribution. For the Gaussian-spring function we also show a linear distribution with
q_{min}\sigma=0.5
.

F.3
Scaling of the observables

After having obtained the scaling function
g(s_{*})
, we can investigate the scaling behaviour of important observables in the jammed- and un-jammed state. We only go quickly through the Langevin case since the Newtonian case (
\xi=0)
is covered in the main text.

The small frequency scenario is considered, where
s\ll\xi
holds. This gives
\omega_{*}=\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{\tau^{2}\xi}

The scaling function is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}g_{\pm}(s\gg\omega_{*})&\to\sqrt{s_{*}}\\
g_{-}(s\ll\omega_{*})&\to 1-s_{*}\\
g_{+}(s\ll\omega_{*})&\to\frac{1}{s_{*}}+1-s_{*}\end{split}

(96)

The dispersion relation is again given by

\displaystyle(v_{q}^{\perp})^{2}=\frac{|\epsilon|}{\tau^{2}h_{q}}

(97)

and we also find
\lambda_{-}^{\perp}\propto 1/\sqrt{\epsilon}.

A density of states cannot be defined in this linear response approach to a dissipative system.

Appendix G
Details on the Euclidean random matrix model and its numerical solutions

Numerical diagonalization

Here, we will give a short overview of the numerical diagonalization of the ERM model. For a more comprehensive discussion we refer to Ref.
[
30
]
, where we studied the ERM model with a Gaussian spring function in detail.

We calculate the underlying random matrix by

\displaystyle\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}}\hskip 1.0pt_{ij}=-m\omega_{0}^{2}f(|\boldsymbol{r}_{i}-\boldsymbol{r}_{j}|)+\delta_{ij}m\omega_{0}^{2}\sum_{k}f(|\boldsymbol{r}_{i}-\boldsymbol{r}_{k}|),

(98)

where
f(r)
is the unitless spring function. The diagonalization of the matrix yields eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}=m\omega_{i}^{2}
and corresponding eigenvectors
\boldsymbol{e}_{i}
from which the density of states
D(\omega)
can be calculated straight forward. In Fig.
6
we show the number of clusters and the number of particles belonging to the largest cluster for different system s. At the rescaled density
n^{*}_{p}=0.85
we observe a distinct drop in the size of the largest cluster and strong This indicates a percolation transition at
n^{*}_{p}=0.838
in agreement with previous studies of overlapping spheres
[
85
,
86
]
. At the same time we observe an extensive number of clusters even above the transition
n^{*}_{p}
. For the
\Theta
-spring function
f^{\Theta}
these unconnected clusters, or rattlers, have integer eigenvalues with a magnitude depending on the number of particles belonging to that cluster. As these eigenvalues appear as spikes in the density of states we remove them from the calculations of
D(\omega)
.

In Fig.
4
we show the scaling of the frequency
\omega^{*}
. This is the frequency where a plateau in the density of states sets in. From the numerical diagonalization, we calculated it as
D(\omega^{*}/\omega_{0})\omega_{0}>D_{\omega^{*}}
, where
D_{\omega^{*}}
is a threshold We chose
D_{\omega^{*}}=0.17
as it approximates the plateau well.
We checked the influence of varying the threshold
D_{\omega}^{*}
and found no qualitative differences.

In Ref.
[
30
]
the dispersion relation
qv_{q}^{\perp}
was obtained by fitting the dynamical structure factor and the velocity auto-correlation function with a damped harmonic oscillator. As the dispersion relation becomes very small approaching the un-jamming transition, we find that these two methods give inaccurate results. In this work we thus calculate the dispersion relation from the
s\to 0
limit of the Laplace transformed velocity autocorrelation function

\displaystyle\hat{K}^{\perp}_{q}(s)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k}\frac{Q_{k}\,s}{s^{2}+\lambda_{k}^{2}},

(99)

where

\displaystyle Q_{k}=\left|\sum_{i}e_{k}^{i}\exp(iqr_{x,i})\right|^{2}

(100)

denotes the projection of the
k
-th eigenvector onto the initial conditions. Note that the normalization in the velocity autocorrelation
1/N
comes from the initial excited plane wave
v_{j}(0)=\exp(iqr_{x,j})
and holds only in the thermodynamic limit (and for uniformly distributed positions
r_{x,j}
).
With this, the dispersion relation is given by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(qv_{q}^{\perp})^{2}}=\lim_{s\to 0}\frac{K(q,s)}{s}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\lambda_{k}>0}\frac{Q_{k}}{\lambda_{k}}.

(101)

This method does not introduce inaccuracies due to binning procedures as it consists of a simple sum over all eigenfrequencies
\omega_{k}
. It should be noted that we only sum over non-zero eigenfrequencies. Numerically, we assume an eigenfrequency to be zero if
\lambda^{k}<10^{-12}
holds.

In the un-jammed phase we are interested in the non-ergodicity parameter
\kappa_{q}
or in other words the finite value to which the velocity autocorrelation function decays for
t\to\infty
. This value is determined by the zero frequency modes. Let
N_{0}
be the number of such modes
k_{0}
. Then we calculate the non-ergodicity parameter by

\displaystyle\kappa^{\perp}_{q}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k_{0}}Q_{k_{0}}

(102)

Numerical solution of the self-consistent equations

To solve our model (for
s=0
), we first calculated the matrix
\underline{\boldsymbol{C}}\hskip 1.0pt
and the high-density correction
\hat{\Delta}_{q}(0)
according to the equations (
36b
) and (
36a
) for each of the considered spring functions. After that, we solved the linear integral equations (
11
) and (
25
) with a simple linear convergence algorithm. For the dispersion relation
qv_{q}^{\perp}
did we chose the bare dispersion
qc_{q}^{\perp}
as the starting value and for the non-ergodicity parameter did we chose
\kappa_{q}=1
as the initial value. In both cases did we let the algorithm run until the distance between two interactions was smaller then
10^{-12}
. All the other observables shown like the Debye-level and the length scale in the un-jammed phase could be calculated with the obtained
v_{q}^{\perp}
and
\kappa_{q}
.

When calculating the one loop correction and the stability matrix, we performed the angular integrals numerically. Since a 6 dimensional integral had to be solved, a numerical error is likely. Indeed, the diagonal elements of the coupling matrix are not necessarily positive anymore for
q\sigma<0.2
. This nonphysical fact can result from the mentioned numerical error or from taking non-diagonal contributions into account. We took 600 wavenumbers
q\sigma=[q_{min}\sigma,60]
into account. We chose the minimal wavenumber for all spring functions considered in such a way that all the diagonal elements of
C_{q,p}
were positive. We checked different distributions of the wavenumbers and considered also even higher
q_{min}
. But this did not affect our results qualitatively nor quantitatively if
q_{min}
was not chosen too large.

The critical eigenvectors
h_{q}
and
\hat{h}_{p}
were obtained via the standard Matlab routine. Having these two variables, we could calculate the observables close to the transition and compare them to the general solutions of
v_{q}^{\perp}
and
\kappa_{q}
obtained via the equations (
11
) and (
25
). We also calculated the value of the plateau appearing in the density of states with
h_{q}
according to equation (
37
).

FIG. 6:
The number of clusters (red symbols, left axis) and the number of particles belonging to the largest cluster (black symbols, right axis) in the ERM model with spring function of finite extent,
f^{\Theta}(r)
, for system sizes
N=10^{3},\,10^{4}
and
N=4\times 10^{4}
.

G.1
Geometric Multiplicity of the eigenvalue zero

We show in this subsection that the Geometric Multiplicity of the eigenvalue
0
equals the number of disconnected clusters if the interaction is purely repulsive
f(r)\geq 0
. This is also confirmed numerically in figure
6
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a partition of
\{1,....,N\}
. We consider a normalised eigenvector
\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
of
\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}}\hskip 1.0pt
with eigenvalue
\lambda
and ask under which condition holds
\lambda=0
. We define the sets
A_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
for
1\leq n\leq M
in the following way:
i,j\in A_{n}
if and only if
\phi_{i}=\phi_{j}
. One has

\displaystyle 2U=\boldsymbol{\phi}^{T}\underline{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}}\hskip 1.0pt\boldsymbol{\phi}=\lambda\;.

(103)

Thus, the energy cost of an eigenmode is zero, if and only if its eigenvalue is zero. We get

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{4U}{m\omega_{0}^{2}}=0&=\sum_{i,j}^{N}\underset{\geq 0}{f(\boldsymbol{r}_{i}-\boldsymbol{r}_{j})}\underset{\geq 0}{(\phi_{i}-\phi_{j})^{2}}\\
&=\sum_{n,m=1}^{M}\sum_{i\in A_{n}}\sum_{j\in A_{m}}f(\boldsymbol{r}_{i}-\boldsymbol{r}_{j})(\phi_{i}-\phi_{j})^{2}\\
&=\sum_{n\neq m}\sum_{i\in A_{n}}\sum_{j\in A_{m}}f(\boldsymbol{r}_{i}-\boldsymbol{r}_{j})\underset{>0}{(\phi_{i}-\phi_{j})^{2}}\;.\end{split}

(104)

Thus,
f(\boldsymbol{r}_{i}-\boldsymbol{r}_{j})=0
has to hold for
i\in A_{n}
and
j\in\{1,...,N\}\subset A_{n}
. Hence, the sets
A_{i}\subset\{1,....,N\}
denote the indices of disconnected clusters and the number
M=\#\mathcal{A}
of disconnected cluster equals the number of linear independent (even orthogonal) eigenvectors with eigenvalue zero. We denote them with
\phi^{(a)}
with
1\leq a\leq M
. They read

\displaystyle\phi^{(a)}\propto\sum_{i\in A_{a}}\hat{e}_{i}\;.

(105)

Here,
\hat{e}_{i}
denotes an element of the standard basis of
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. Note, that the assumed translational invariance implies that
M\geq 1
holds.

As a consequence, finite restoring forces in the limit
q\to 0
in the ERM-model can only exists if a cluster percolates the system. The speed of sound
v_{q}^{\perp}
is only non-zero if the
n>n_{c}
, where
n_{c}
is the percolation threshold.
